## Page 4

4
This is the [RE]action System by Monster 
Fight Club®. This section will go over the 
basics of the system and its tools.
The [RE]action System is scenario-driven. 
These rules cover one-off games as 
well as playing a series of games in a 
campaign. In each scenario, the players 
will each control a team of miniatures on 
the table. 
[RE]ACTION SYSTEMTM
The [RE]action System uses three 
different colors of dice which link to three 
colors of actions that models can take. 
There are also colors of dice linked to 
other types of rolls, such as the Obstacle 
die.
ACTIONS
ACTIONS
When it’s your turn to play, you’ll be taking 
actions with your models. Actions include 
moving, attacking, healing, and scenario-
specific actions. 
Each model has a number of actions 
available to them, colored GREEN
GREEN, 
YELLOW
YELLOW, or RED
RED. You’ll use Action 
tokens to track how many actions each 
Character has available.
When one of your models takes an action, 
they’ll flip the corresponding Action token, 
to show it’s been used.
The color of the Action token used 
determines what color die you’ll roll, 
to see if the action succeeds or fails. 
Generally, GREEN
GREEN actions are the best, 
then YELLOW
YELLOW, with RED 
 RED actions being 
least likely to succeed.
OPPOSED DICE ROLLS
OPPOSED DICE ROLLS
When you attempt an action, your 
opponent gets to make an Opposed Roll. 
If your roll is higher than theirs, the action 
succeeds. Otherwise, the action fails.
[RE]ACTIONS
[RE]ACTIONS
If one of your Characters is wounded by a 
rival’s attack, you’ll have the opportunity 
to react to it with an out-of-turn action.
THE DICE
Combat Zone primarily uses four different 
colors of dice. 
THE ACTION DICE
THE ACTION DICE
Three of the dice colors are called Action 
dice. They are rolled to see if a model’s 
action succeeds or not. These are the 
GREEN
GREEN, YELLOW
YELLOW, and RED 
 RED dice.
You’ll roll these colored Action dice 
throughout the game when taking actions 
with your models.
THE OBSTACLE DIE
THE OBSTACLE DIE
The black ten-sided dice are called 
Obstacle dice, and are used when models 
attempt something that doesn’t target 
a rival, such as climbing or 
attempting to hack a terminal. 
Models are considered “the 
defender” when rolling against 
the Obstacle die, and win in the case of 
a tie.
OTHER DICE
OTHER DICE
There are other rare types of dice used in 
the [RE]action system (such as a purple 
d4). The rules will explain how to use 
these dice when needed.
CRIT & FUMBLE SYMBOLS
CRIT & FUMBLE SYMBOLS
Each die also has a CRIT as its highest 
value and a FUMBLE in place of the 1.
Crits usually mean success, and can 
trigger special rules. 
Fumbles usually mean you have failed 
whatever you’re attempting, and can 
trigger worse results.
INTRODUCTION
GREEN
GREEN
Twelve-Sided (d12) 
YELLOW
YELLOW
Eight-Sided (d8)
RED
RED
Six-Sided (d6)
OBSTACLE
OBSTACLE
Ten-Sided (d10) 
Crit
Fumble

## Page 5

5
ACTION DICE
ACTION DICE
THE LIMITER
Combat Zone uses the “The Limiter” to measure 
distances during the game. The Limiter is divided 
into three ranges: GREEN
GREEN, YELLOW
YELLOW, and RED
RED:
REACH 
REACH 
The Limiter is 1” wide. Some actions and rules, 
such as Brawling, apply to models “within reach” of 
another model. A model is within reach when it is 
within the Limiter’s 1” width.
MODELS
Models are the representations of your team 
members on the board. Your models are called 
“friendly” models and those belonging to your 
opponent are referred to as “rival” models.
Models are mechanically defined in two parts: 
the figure and the base. The figure is the image or 
sculpture of a combatant which determines which 
card and abilities the model uses in-game. The base 
is a round disk at the bottom, which keeps the model 
upright and is used for measuring distances and 
position mechanics. The standard [RE]action base is 
27mm in diameter, but some models may be issued 
with larger bases if the model requires.
CARD SUPREMACY
If a rule on a card ever contradicts the rules in the 
rulebook, the rule on the card takes precedence. 
Special rules on cards may allow you to move 
different distances, attack targets you wouldn’t 
normally be allowed to, or otherwise “break” the rules 
in specific ways.

## Page 6

6
There are three things you must do to prepare for a game in the [RE}action system: 
build your team, choose a scenario, and construct a Battlespace.
BUILDING A TEAM
Before the game, you’ll hire your team, consisting of a mixture of model types. You 
can also enhance your Characters by purchasing extra gear or other enhancements 
(such as programs in Cyberpunk RED: Combat Zone). 
SIZE OF GAMES
SIZE OF GAMES
Games in the [RE]action system can be played in about 45 minutes to an hour at 
competitive levels (100 to 150 EB in Cyberpunk RED: Combat Zone). Larger games 
(200+ points) are played on a larger table and take longer to play.
•	 100 points – 22” x 30”
•	 150 points – 22” x 30” 
•	 200 points – 44” x 30” 
CHOOSING A SCENARIO
Games in the [RE]action system are played using Scenarios. Each of the scenarios 
describes a different combat situation with its own goals, special rules, and setup. 
If you are playing a campaign, winning or losing may have consequences in 
future games.
WINNING A SCENARIO
WINNING A SCENARIO
Different scenarios have different goals, which might range from taking out rival 
models, stealing assets, capturing territory, or just about any other deed that needs 
doing. Each Scenario will define how a player scores points towards victory.
CONSTRUCTING A BATTLESPACE
The area your models will fight and move through is the Battlespace. Before you 
play, you must set up the Battlespace using scenery, guided by the instructions for 
the scenario. A miniatures game can be played on a variety of terrain tables and 
gaming boards. 
IT IS VITALLY IMPORTANT TO DISCUSS THE TERRAIN WITH YOUR OPPONENT WHILE 
IT IS VITALLY IMPORTANT TO DISCUSS THE TERRAIN WITH YOUR OPPONENT WHILE 
SETTING UP, DEFINING OBSTACLES AND BARRIERS (PAGE 21) AND WHAT CAN BE 
SETTING UP, DEFINING OBSTACLES AND BARRIERS (PAGE 21) AND WHAT CAN BE 
DRAGGED (PAGE 20).
DRAGGED (PAGE 20).
After you’ve constructed the Battlespace, follow any other instructions in the setup for 
the scenario. Once Setup is completed, you’re ready to start the game. The scenario 
will tell you which player has control first.
CAMPAIGN PLAY
CAMPAIGN PLAY
Combat Zone is excellent as a one-off game, but there are campaign rules as well. 
These can be found starting on page 36.
You’ll have the chance to promote Characters to veterans and acquire new gear or 
enhancements. The campaign plays different every time as you move towards the 
final mission. 
PREPARING TO PLAY

## Page 7

7
BATTLESPACE DEFINITIONS
Scenarios use a variety of terms to indicate locations 
on the Battlespace. This is most common when 
defining team deployment areas or the placement of 
objectives and other scenario specific elements. This 
example assumes a 22” x 30” Battlespace/board.
BATTLESPACE / BOARD
BATTLESPACE / BOARD
The terms Battlespace and Board may be used 
interchangeably, and both refer to the playing 
surface. This area is typically flat, but some terrain 
tables may have elevation elements built in. The 
Battlespace definitions assume the surface is 
flat, and ignore any height when determining the 
following locations.
EDGES (LONG & SHORT)
EDGES (LONG & SHORT)
The four lines where the Battlespace ends are the 
board edges. The long board edges are the parallel 
edges longest in length (either 30”). The short board 
edges are the parallel lines shortest in length (either 
22”). If you’re playing on a non-standard board size, 
the players may need to define the board edges 
during setup.
CENTERLINE
CENTERLINE
There are two centerlines, one long and one short. 
The long centerline runs parallel to the long board 
edges exactly halfway between them (11”). The short 
centerline runs parallel to the short board edges 
exactly halfway between them (15”). 
CENTER
CENTER
The center of the Battlespace is the point at the 
middle of the board. The center is located at the 
point that is 11” from both long board edges and 15” 
from both short board edges. The center is marked 
with a green star in the above example.
QUARTER
QUARTER
There are four quarters. Each quarter is 11” x 15” 
and do not overlap. Each quarter also has a center 
(the center of each board quarter is marked with a 
red X in the above example. A quarter’s center is the 
point 5.5” along the quarter’s short edge and 7.5” 
along the quarter’s long edge.
LONG EDGE
LONG EDGE
LONG EDGE
LONG EDGE
SHORT EDGE
SHORT EDGE
SHORT EDGE
SHORT EDGE
LONG CENTERLINE
LONG CENTERLINE
SHORT CENTERLINE
SHORT CENTERLINE

## Page 8

8
CHARACTER CARDS
Each Character model has an associated 
Character card that defines how it performs.
NAME (A)
NAME (A)
The name is used to identify the Character. 
KEYWORDS (B)
KEYWORDS (B)
Keywords define how a Character interacts 
with certain rules.
VETERAN STATUS (C)
VETERAN STATUS (C)
Veteran and elite Characters have a number 
of stars to the right of their name. Veteran’s 
have one star, and Elites have two stars.
COST (D)
COST (D)
The cost to include this Character in your 
team.
SKILLS (E)
SKILLS (E)
Most models have three skills. When taking 
an action models add their relevant skill value 
(if any) to the die roll. The six skills are Melee, 
Ranged, Reflexes, Tech, Influence, and Medical.
ACTIONS (F)
ACTIONS (F)
Each card shows how many actions a model has and of what color those actions are at 
the start of the game. Actions can be GREEN
GREEN, YELLOW
YELLOW, or RED
RED.
SPECIAL RULES (G)
SPECIAL RULES (G)
Many Characters have a special rule or action listed on their card. This rule applies only 
to them, unless stated otherwise. If the special rule is an action, it will show a skill icon or 
state that it is an action. Otherwise, it is an ability and does not require an action to use.
GEAR (H)
GEAR (H)
Many models have gear on the bottom of their card which gives them new abilities or 
actions. This gear may be looted by other Characters.
GEAR CARDS
Character may equip Gear cards.
NAME (A)
NAME (A)
The name of the gear. A model may not 
equip more than one gear of the same 
name.
KEYWORDS (B)
KEYWORDS (B)
Keywords define how the gear interacts.
RARITY (C)
RARITY (C)
The maximum number of copies of this 
card that a team may include. If a card’s 
rarity is listed as Universal, the rarity stacks.
RULES (D)
RULES (D)
The model’s special actions or abilities are 
listed here.
COST (E)
COST (E)
The cost to equip this card to a model.
STREET CRED REQUIREMENT (F)
STREET CRED REQUIREMENT (F)
The total amount of Street Cred a team 
must have in order to add this card to the 
HQ.

## Page 9

GONK CARDS
Cyberpunk RED:Combat Zone uses a special model called a Gonk. 
Gonks of the same name share a single card. Some teams may have 
different types of Gonks to choose from. Different teams’ Gonks will 
have different names, but they will all have the Gonk keyword.
GONKS VS. CHARACTERS
GONKS VS. CHARACTERS
The rules refer to “models,” which include both Gonks and Characters. 
If a rule uses “Character,” then it can’t be used by or affect Gonks. 
This includes things like gear (Gonks cannot be equipped with 
additional gear or other enhancements).
HIRING GONKS 
HIRING GONKS 
The maximum number of Gonks you can include in your team is the 
total combined Influence of all your Characters. The cost is per Gonk.

## Page 10

10
Now that the scenario is reviewed, the 
Battlespace is built, and the teams 
deployed, the player’s will begin taking 
turns.
BATTLEFIELD CONTROL
At any one time, only one player will 
have control of the Battlespace. The 
player in control takes actions with their 
models, moving through the Battlespace, 
attacking enemies, and otherwise 
attempting to achieve the scenario goals. 
When a player takes control, they choose 
to either Activate a Character or to Inspire 
Their Team.
ACTIVATE A CHARACTER
ACTIVATE A CHARACTER
When you activate a Character, you 
choose one of your Characters: they are 
now your active model. While you have 
control, you may take actions with your 
active model.
Control passes to your rival when you 
cannot, or do not want to, take any more 
actions with your active model. You can 
take as few or as many of a model’s 
actions as you wish.
When a model is granted a single action 
(as a [RE]action or by an ability) the 
active model does not change. The model 
granted the action simply takes the action 
as defined by the ability (like taking a free 
move action or a [RE]action).
BREAKING CONTROL
BREAKING CONTROL
Actions that allow you to activate another 
model are called Command actions. They 
can alter the flow of turns, and cause new 
models to become the active model (see 
page 19).
INSPIRE YOUR TEAM
INSPIRE YOUR TEAM
When all your Action tokens are used, 
or you wish to refresh your team’s used 
Action tokens, you Inspire Your Team 
instead of Activating a Character. When 
you Inspire Your Team (sometimes called 
Inspire on cards), you activate your Gonks 
and refresh your Characters’ Action 
tokens. 
First, you activate all of your Gonks. When 
a Gonk activates, it takes a single action. 
You can activate your Gonks in any order 
you choose. 
After your Gonks activate, refresh all of 
your Characters’ used Action tokens by 
flipping them to their ready side. 
Finally, you resolve some cleanup. If you 
have Hacked tokens on your Characters 
when you Inspire, you remove one Hacked 
token from each of your models (page 
29). Any exhausted cards also refresh, 
and if you have any Difficult actions, they 
can be used again (page 11).
After cleanup, your rival takes control.
WHEN MUST YOU INSPIRE  
WHEN MUST YOU INSPIRE  
OR ACTIVATE? 
OR ACTIVATE? 
If none of your Characters have ready 
Action tokens, you must Inspire Your 
Team. If all your Characters’ Action 
tokens are ready, you must activate a 
Character. 
TURNS AND ACTIONS

## Page 11

11
TAKING ACTIONS
Action tokens are used to track a model’s actions. 
They show a Model’s number of actions, of what 
color, and whether those actions are ready or used. 
When a Character takes an action, choose one of 
its ready Action tokens and flip it to its used side. 
This is important, since the color of the Action token 
spent determines the color die roll when taking the 
action, or the distance the model moves.
STARTING ACTION TOKENS
STARTING ACTION TOKENS
Each Character begins the game with the Action 
tokens shown on their card. Actions tokens begin the 
game ready. Place them on or near the card, so that 
it’s clear which tokens belong to which Character.
AVAILABLE ACTIONS
AVAILABLE ACTIONS
When a model is active, they can attempt actions. 
Characters may choose from a variety of actions, 
including basic actions (page 14), actions granted 
by cards, and scenario actions. Models may take 
the same action multiple times, (unless the action is 
Difficult). 
ATTACK ACTIONS
ATTACK ACTIONS
Attack actions may only target rival models and, if 
they are successful, they deal a wound to the target. 
Any action with the attack symbol (shown here) is 
an attack action. This icon may appear next to the 
attack’s text or attached to the attack’s skill icon.
FULLY RESOLVING ACTIONS
FULLY RESOLVING ACTIONS
Each action must be fully resolved before a model 
may start another action. If an event triggers another 
action (either on the acting model or another model) 
then finish resolving the current action before 
moving on to the triggered action.
FREE ACTIONS & MOVEMENT
FREE ACTIONS & MOVEMENT
When an ability or effect causes a model to take 
an action “for free” the model does not need to use 
an Action token to take the action. To determine 
the color of the action, the model may choose any 
Action token it has, ready or used (Gonks use the 
action color on their card). When an ability causes a 
model to move, this movement is also free (see page 
21).
EASY & DIFFICULT ACTIONS
EASY & DIFFICULT ACTIONS
EASY
EASY actions don’t require a skill roll, they 
automatically succeed (an Action token is still used).
Normally, models choose any combination of 
available actions when they activate, including 
the same action multiple times. The exception is 
DIFFICULT
DIFFICULT actions. 
Difficult actions can be used once and can only be 
used again after you Inspire Your Team.
When you use a Difficult action, place a used Action 
token on it as a reminder that it cannot be used. 
When you Inspire Your Team, remove the Action 
token to show that it is available again.
EXHAUSTING CARDS
EXHAUSTING CARDS
When you are directed to “exhaust” a card you must 
flip the card over so it’s back is showing. When you 
refresh your team, you can also refresh (flip face-up) 
any exhausted cards.
GONK ACTIONS
GONK ACTIONS
Gonks take a single action whenever they’re 
activated and don’t use Action tokens. 
Gonks may take any of the basic actions, except the 
basic Influence action (Command).
Since Gonks don’t use Action tokens each Gonk 
model may take one action when you Inspire Your 
Team or when other models force them to act. 
They’ll always roll their action die (usually YELLOW
YELLOW) 
and if they suffer a wound, they’re taken out.

## Page 12

12
RESOLVING AN ACTION
After choosing an action and using an Action token, 
you’ll do the following to resolve the action:
•	 CHOOSE THE ACTION’S TARGET:
CHOOSE THE ACTION’S TARGET: Make sure it’s 
within the action’s range and there is a Path of 
Attack (see page 24).
•	 DETERMINE OPPOSITION:
DETERMINE OPPOSITION: If the target is a rival, 
they choose which Action token they will use for 
the Opposed roll. If the action does not target 
a rival model, the rival player will use the black 
Obstacle die for the Opposed roll.
•	 ROLL DICE:
ROLL DICE: Both players roll their dice at the same 
time and add the appropriate skill value, plus any 
other modifiers (such as cover, page 24).
•	 COMPARE RESULTS:
COMPARE RESULTS: If the acting Model’s total is 
higher, the action succeeds; otherwise it fails.
When a model attempts an action, you’ll roll a die to 
resolve whether the action succeeds or fails. You’ll 
roll the color die that matches the Action token used 
to attempt the action. 
When an action targets a rival, the target rival 
chooses a die for their Opposed roll that matches 
any one of their Action tokens; it don’t matter if 
they’re ready or used. The target doesn’t have to 
spend the token, but one of its tokens must be 
chosen to oppose the action.
If the action doesn’t target a rival model, the rival player 
rolls the black, ten-sided Obstacle die and does not add 
any skill to the roll. This means that actions without a 
target (like moving over an obstacle) or that target your 
own friendly models (like the basic Tech action) must 
roll against the Obstacle die to succeed
SUCCESS & FAILURE
SUCCESS & FAILURE
If the action is an attack, and it succeeds, then the 
target will suffer a wound (page 16). If the action 
isn’t an attack, then the action will define the results 
of success. Typically, if an action fails, it does not 
resolve the success result, but if anything else 
occurs the action will define the failure result.
(UN)SKILLED ROLLS
(UN)SKILLED ROLLS
Every model can use any action available to them, 
even if the skill doesn’t appear on their Character card: 
they simply have “0” bonus. 
CRITS & FUMBLES
The dice have two special results: 
Crit and Fumble.
CRIT: 
CRIT: A Crit will always succeed, 
regardless of any modifiers or 
bonuses, unless your opponent also 
rolls a Crit. When both players roll a 
Crit it is considered a tie, and a tie 
goes to the defender.
FUMBLE:
FUMBLE: When you roll a Fumble, you 
always fail, regardless of what your 
opponent rolled or any bonuses or the 
type of die. When both players roll a 
Fumble it is considered a tie, and so 
the defender wins.
GETTING LUCKY
Each team starts with three Luck tokens by default. 
You can spend your Luck tokens at any time to re-roll 
a single die roll that you rolled (including the Obstacle 
dice that you roll). You can’t re-roll your rival’s dice 
(including Obstacle dice rolled by your rival). 
You also can’t re-roll the same die roll more than once, 
even if the reroll is worse.
When both players might use a Luck token, the order 
is resolved in this manner:
•	 The player who has control must decide if they will 
re-roll first. 
•	 Then, their rival may choose to re-roll, after seeing 
the result of the controlling player’s re-roll (if any). 
•	 If the rival does re-roll, and the controlling player 
didn’t re-roll initially, the controlling player can 
spend a Luck to re-roll now (after seeing the rival’s 
re-roll result).
LUCKY STREAK
LUCKY STREAK
If you spend a Luck token and the  
re-roll is a Crit, take the Luck token back.
UNDERDOG’S LUCK
UNDERDOG’S LUCK
If your team’s Street Cred is lower 
than your opponent’s, you start the 
game with extra Luck tokens. For 
each point of Street Cred your rival 
has more than you, you get an extra 
Luck token.
FUMBLE
FUMBLE

SKILLS & BASIC ACTIONS
Every model on your team has six skills. If a model’s Character card does not list a 
skill, the model has a bonus of 0 with that skill. Each of the skills has a basic action 
that any model can take. 
MELEE SKILL & ACTIONS
The Melee skill covers raw physical power, as well as its effective application. It is most 
often used for melee attacks, but it may also be used by actions that require strength.
The basic Melee attack action uses a model’s Melee skill in an attempt to wound a 
rival target within RED
RED. 
MELEE ACTIONS ARE OPPOSED BY THE TARGET’S MELEE SKILL. 
MELEE ACTIONS ARE OPPOSED BY THE TARGET’S MELEE SKILL. 
BASIC MELEE ATTACK
BASIC MELEE ATTACK
RANGED SKILL & ACTIONS
The Ranged skill covers physical accuracy. It is most often used for ranged attacks, 
but it may also be used by actions that require precision.
The basic Ranged attack action uses a model’s Ranged skill in an attempt to wound a 
rival target within YELLOW 
 YELLOW or GREEN 
 GREEN range. Note, it cannot target rivals at RED 
 RED range. 
RANGED ACTIONS ARE OPPOSED BY THE TARGET’S REFLEXES SKILL. 
RANGED ACTIONS ARE OPPOSED BY THE TARGET’S REFLEXES SKILL. 
BASIC RANGED ATTACK
BASIC RANGED ATTACK
REFLEXES SKILL & ACTIONS
The Reflexes skill covers speed and agility. It is used to traverse terrain and by actions 
that require acrobatics and leaping.
The basic Move action moves your model along the Limiter a distance that matches 
the color of the action used (RED
RED, YELLOW
YELLOW, or GREEN
GREEN). If their path is clear of 
obstacles, the action is Easy. Moving over obstacles requires a Reflexes roll against 
the Obstacle die (page 21).
REFLEX ACTIONS ARE OPPOSED BY THE TARGET’S REFLEXES SKILL.
REFLEX ACTIONS ARE OPPOSED BY THE TARGET’S REFLEXES SKILL.
BASIC MOVE ACTION
BASIC MOVE ACTION
Move up to your action color. If the move is unobstructed,  
this action is Easy (it does not require a skill roll).

Action token. 
When a Character is wounded during 
an Opposed Roll (such as resisting an

their Opposed Roll is the one changed to 
a RED

If a Character is wounded as a result of 
an action they take (such as 
jumping a gap or fumbling 
a Dangerous action), then 
the Action token used 
on that action is the one 
changed to a RED 
 RED token. 
The new RED 
 RED token 
comes into play with the 
same side up as the one 
it replaced.

EXAMPLE: The Oyabun is targeted by

 YELLOW 
token for his Opposed Roll.

replace the chosen YELLOW

YELLOW

 RED

TAKING OUT YOUR RIVALS
RED

when suffering a wound, and the model

with a RED 
 RED Action token and is wounded.

token in base contact with the model and

To remember which body belongs to

## Page 18

[RE]ACTIONS
When a Character takes a wound from an attack, that Character may immediately 
make a [RE]action. [RE]actions use Action tokens like normal actions do, so the 
Character must have a ready Action token in order to use a [RE]action.
[RE]actions are a single action, taken after your rival’s wounding action is fully 
resolved. They interrupt the normal sequence of your opponent taking actions.
Each model may only [RE]act once per enemy action, even if that action causes more 
than one wound (such as a Rapid attack). If that Character is wounded again by a 
different action (and has another ready Action token), then they may [RE]act again.
YOU MAY NOT [RE]ACT TO A [RE]ACTION! 
YOU MAY NOT [RE]ACT TO A [RE]ACTION! There are some other situations in which 
a model cannot [RE]act: If the Character is taken out by an attack’s wound, it may 
not [RE]act. If a Character suffers a wound that was not dealt by an attack, then it 
may not [RE]act. 
[RE]ACTION ATTACKS
[RE]ACTION ATTACKS
If you take an attack with a [RE]action, the attack must target the model who dealt the 
wound. If the [RE]action attack targets multiple models, like a Torrent attack (page 33), 
then the model that caused the wound must be one of those target models.
UNREACTIVE GONKS 
UNREACTIVE GONKS 
GONKS DON’T TAKE [RE]ACTIONS. 
GONKS DON’T TAKE [RE]ACTIONS. Gonks do not have Action tokens and are taken out 
when they suffer a single wound, so they don’t ever make [RE]actions. 
[RE]ACTIONS & TIMING

wound with her Melee attack. The Ripper replaces

 YELLOW token with a RED
RED.

Move action. He moves by spending his RED 
 RED 
Action token, and is now out of range of the 
Oyabun’s follow up attacks.

## Page 19

TRIGGER TIMING
Some abilities will trigger when an event occurs 
(such as “After this model Activates” or “When 
this model is taken out”). These events happen 
immediately when the triggering event first occurs. 
However, if the event trigger generates an action, 
that action is resolved after any current action 
finishes resolving but before any other actions are 
resolved (including [RE]actions).
If two or more events are triggered at the exact same 
time (such as “When you Inspire Your Team”), the 
players resolve the events in the following order:
•	 First, the Activate player resolves all the events 
under their control in any order they wish.
•	 Next, the Rival player resolves all the events under 
their control in any order they wish.
•	 Finally, if any events are not controlled by a player 
(for instance those generated by the Scenario), 
those events are resolved in an order determined 
by the Active player. 
CONTROL
The player who is taking their turn has control. 
When the controlling player has finished activating 
a Character or Inspiring Their Team, control passes 
to their rival. Control can be stolen with Command 
actions.
[RE]ACTION CONTROL
[RE]ACTION CONTROL
[RE]actions do not normally change the active Model 
and do not change who has control. However, if you 
take out the rival active Model with a [RE]action, your 
opponent loses control. 
In addition, some command actions do change who 
is the active Model. If one of these actions is used 
as a [RE]action, control may pass to a new player. 
Actions which change who has control may not be 
taken while your opponent is Inspiring Their Team 
(such as the basic Influence action, Command). 
If you take out a Model while your opponent is 
Inspiring Their Team, they retain control. A PLAYER 
A PLAYER 
MAY NEVER LOSE CONTROL WHILE THEY INSPIRE 
MAY NEVER LOSE CONTROL WHILE THEY INSPIRE 
THEIR TEAM.
THEIR TEAM.
COMMAND ACTIONS
COMMAND ACTIONS
Actions that activate another model, like the basic 
Influence action, are called command actions. 
Command actions can be used as [RE]actions, 
unless otherwise stated. They can also be used to 
maintain control by changing the currently active 
model to a model with ready Action tokens.
You can only ever activate Characters who have at 
least one ready Action token. If all of a Characters’ 
Action tokens are used, they may not be the target 
of a command action. 
If the Command action is successful, the target 
model becomes the active Model and the Model 
who successfully took the command action ends 
their activation. Control passes to the player who 
owns the newly activated model (if that is the same 
player, control does not change). 
Command actions usually (but not always) use the 
Influence skill, and allow you to activate a friendly 
model. Like other actions that target a friendly 
model, they are opposed by the Obstacle die. 
There are some command actions that allow you 
to activate a rival. In this case, they are opposed 
by the target’s Influence. The newly activated rival 
is still controlled by your opponent: but you get to 
dictate who will be the next active Model. 
Only the target of the command action may become 
the active Model. This means the player who owns 
the newly activated model may not choose to Inspire 
Their Team or activate some other Character.
COMMAND ACTIONS & GONKS
COMMAND ACTIONS & GONKS
Gonks don’t need a ready Action token to activate, 
(they don’t have Action tokens), so you can still 
target them with command actions. If a Gonk 
becomes the active model, it resolves actions as 
normal. Since no Action tokens are spent, the Gonk 
may still take actions when the controlling player 
Inspires Their Team.

## Page 21

OBSTACLES & BARRIERS
There are two types of scenery: obstacles and 
barriers. Obstacles hinder movement but don’t 
stop it, like fences and cars. A barrier, is usually 
impassible, like concrete walls. Impassable objects 
taller than RED 
 RED are barriers.
Before a game, discuss what scenery falls into which 
category with your rival. 
MOVING & OBSTACLES
MOVING & OBSTACLES
When a model’s path crosses an obstacle, the move 
action requires a Reflexes roll (same color as the 
action) opposed by the Obstacle die.
If the move action’s roll is successful, the model 
cleared the obstacle. It can continue moving. If it 
failed, they must stop where they first encountered 
the obstacle.
MOVING THROUGH RIVAL MODELS
MOVING THROUGH RIVAL MODELS
Rivals are an obstacle. When moving through a 
rival model, your Reflexes roll is opposed by their 
Reflexes, instead of the Obstacle die. If successful, 
the active Model keeps moving. If it fails, the model 
stops within reach of the rival model! They are now 
in a Brawl (see page 26).
PARKOUR!
PARKOUR!
A model may need to make multiple rolls during a 
single move action if they move through multiple 
obstacles or rival models. Each obstacle requires a 
separate roll. With a GREEN 
 GREEN action, a model may 
encounter several obstacles along the way!
FREE MOVEMENT
FREE MOVEMENT
When an ability causes a model to move, this is 
shorthand for granting the model a free basic move 
action. If the move effect has no color is specified, 
the distance is determined by the Action token 
chosen. If the move has a color specified, the 
moving model must still choose one of its Action 
tokens, which determines what die is rolled during 
parkour, but the distance moved is determined by 
the effect’s text. 
The Ronin is taking a 
GREEN 
GREEN basic Move 
action to move the 
full distance of the 
limiter.
This Obstacle is in the 
way, so the Ronin must 
succeed on a Reflexes 
roll vs. the Obstacle die 
to clear it.
Finally, the Ronin’s path requires him 
to move out of reach range of the 
Maelstrom model (see page 24), so 
it must also pass a Reflexes roll vs. 
the Maelstrom’s Reflexes roll to keep 
moving.

22
BARRIERS & VERTICALITY
A Battlespace may include tall structures that require 
vertical movement.
MOVING & BARRIERS
MOVING & BARRIERS
Models can’t move through barriers – models must go 
around or over the barrier. If a barrier is RED 
 RED or shorter it 
may be moved over using Parkour (page 21), if it is taller 
than RED 
 RED it must be climbed (page 23).
OPENINGS IN BARRIERS
OPENINGS IN BARRIERS
Most openings (like holes in a wall or open windows) are 
treated as obstacles that a model can draw paths through. 
Some buildings may be so full of openings that they might 
be defined as areas of obstacle, not barriers.
A door or similar intended opening allows a model through 
without a Reflexes roll.
FALLING DAMAGE
FALLING DAMAGE
When a model falls, use the Limiter to measure the 
distance fallen, stopping at the next horizontal surface 
below the model. If the distance fallen is within the RED 
 RED 
range, the model suffers no wounds. If they fall within the 
YELLOW 
YELLOW or GREEN 
 GREEN range, the model takes 1 wound. If you 
fall more than GREEN 
 GREEN range the model is taken out. 
If an object (or model) falls onto a model on a lower level, 
the model on the lower level suffers falling damage as if it 
had fallen the same distance as the object. The object is 
then placed into base-to-base contact with the model on 
the lower level.
A model may not choose to fall as part of a move, the 
model must attempt to traverse (by jumping a gap or 
climbing). Falling happens when movement or another 
affect leaves a model in a position where it cannot stand.
JUMPING GAPS
JUMPING GAPS

must make a Reflexes roll vs. a die the same color as 
the gap’s distance. Succeed, and the model completes 
the move action normally. Fail, and your model has 
miscalculated its leap and fallen. A model’s base must be 
able to land fully on the other side of a gap to jump it.

CLIMBING UP & DOWN
To climb up or down a vertical barrier taller than RED 
 RED during its 
movement, a model must succeed at a Reflexes roll against the 
Obstacle die. These rules use the term “climb” but this is short 
hand for a Character using various skills to traverse vertically.
CLIMBING – SINGLE ACTION
CLIMBING – SINGLE ACTION
Models may move vertically further than RED 
 RED by measuring their 
movement directly, from their starting position to the elevated 
position along an angled path or along a curved limiter path.
Make a Reflexes roll opposed by the Obstacle die. If successful, 
the model has climbed the barrier as part of their movement. The 
model may continue moving after reaching the top or bottom of 
the barrier and climb up and down in the same action, as long as 
the total movement is within the action color’s limit. Like parkour, each 
vertical traversal requires its own Reflexes roll against the Obstacle die.
If the action fails, the model falls from the highest point of their 
movement. 
A model climbing outside of its activation (such as a granted free 
move action or forced movement by a rival) may climb as part of the 
movement, but the model will fall from the highest point of its movement 
if it cannot reach a legal standing position.
CLIMBING – MULTIPLE ACTIONS
CLIMBING – MULTIPLE ACTIONS
Models may end an action partially up a vertical barrier. If a height is too 
tall to traverse in a single action, a model may take multiple basic move 
actions in a row to climb. When an active model ends a move mid-climb 
(where it cannot stand) it may take additional move actions to climb the 
remaining distance. Each move requires its own Reflexes roll. 
If the model’s activation ends or it attempts a non-move action while it is 
mid-climb, then it immediately falls (before any actions or [RE]actions). 
If a model can [RE]Act to the climb, it may 
not do so until the active model finishes 
climbing (or falls).
EASY CLIMBS
EASY CLIMBS
Some barriers may be easy to climb (like a 
ladder). Models may move up or down the 
barrier without making a Reflexes roll, as 
long as they move along a path taking the 
ladder (or other easy climbing surface).

GREEN basic move, just barely

PATH OF ATTACK
Many actions target another 
model: friendly or rival. For an 
action to target a model, the active 
Model must have a Path of Attack 
to the target. This path might be 
obscured (making the actions 
more difficult). 
To draw a Path of Attack, imagine 
a path the width of the Limiter 
(1”) from the acting model’s base 
to the target’s base: this is the 
Path of Attack. 
VISIBLE
VISIBLE
When two models can draw a 
Path of Attack to each other, they 
are visible to each other.
OBSTACLES IN THE PATH 
OBSTACLES IN THE PATH 
Each obstacle that intersects or 
fully crosses a Path of Attack adds 
+1 to the target’s Opposed Roll. 
Other models (friendly and rival) 
count as obstacles. This means 
you add +1 to the Opposed Roll for 
each model in the Path of Attack. 
Objects, like barricades and 
barrels, are obstacles. But, an 
object within reach of the attacker 
doesn’t affect the Opposed Roll. 
BARRIERS IN THE PATH
BARRIERS IN THE PATH
Barriers that enter your Path 
of Attack but do not fully cross it 
add +1 to the Opposed Roll just like 
obstacles.
If a barrier fully crosses your Path 
of Attack, the Path of Attack is 
blocked, the model’s are not visible 
to one another, and you may not 
take the action against that target. 
TERRAIN DEFINITIONS
TERRAIN DEFINITIONS
There are an infinite number of 
terrain boards that can be played 
on. It is important to discuss terrain 
definitions with your rival while 
setting up the game.

Path of Attack to the Ripper.
The Oyabun’s Path of

a window, which is an 
obstacle. The Ripper 
benefits from a +1 to 
his Opposed roll.
A barrier enters 
the Path of 
Attack but 
doesn’t fully 
block it. This 
obscures the 
target, for a +1 
to the Ripper’s 
Opposed Roll.
This barrier fully 
crosses the Path 
of Attack — the 
Oyabun cannot 
make attack’s 
against the 
Ripper.

MULTIPLE TARGETS
When an action targets more than one 
model, the attacker makes a single die roll, 
which is opposed by all the targeted models, 
who each make their own Opposed Roll. 
This can result in an attack that wounds one 
model but misses another.
This happens most often with Torrent 
attacks (page 33) and when models shoot 
at Brawling models (page 26).
EXAMPLE:
EXAMPLE: The Ripper’s Torrent attack may 
target all models within a chosen Path of 
Attack (as shown on the right).
Since the action has a YELLOW 
 YELLOW range, the 
two closer Tyger Claw models would both 
be targeted by the attack, but the third 
model (the Oyabun) in GREEN 
 GREEN range would 
not.
Both targeted Tyger Claw models make 
their own Opposed Roll. Each model whose 
Opposed Roll is lower than the Ripper’s roll 
will be wounded by the attack.
ELEVATED ATTACKS
Any barrier taller than RED 
 RED that a model can stand on is considered “elevated”. When a model is within reach 
of the edge of an elevated barrier they are standing on (such as the edge of a rooftop), that barrier counts as 
an obstacle if they are attacked, but not when they are attacking.
Here, because the Ronin 
is not within reach of the 
barrier edge, neither of the 
models would have a Path 
of Attack to each other. 
Here, the edge of the building counts 
as an obstacle to the Maelstrom Ripper 
below, but the Ronin on the roof has a 
clear Path of Attack to the model below.
This is because the 
building is treated as a 
normal barrier since the 
model on top is not within 
reach of the edge, and it 
completely crosses the 
Path of Attack. 
SAMPLE TORRENT ATTACK
SAMPLE TORRENT ATTACK
Target all models within the action’s 
Path of Attack.

When two rival models’ bases are within reach of each other, 
they are “Brawling” with each other. Friendly models are never 
brawling with each other.
TARGETS WHILE BRAWLING
BRAWLING MODELS MAY ONLY TARGET RIVALS THEY ARE 
BRAWLING MODELS MAY ONLY TARGET RIVALS THEY ARE 
BRAWLING WITH.
BRAWLING WITH. This means many actions aren’t usable in 
Brawls. If an action can’t be used within RED 
 RED or reach range, 
then it cannot be used. Model’s also can’t take any action that 
targets friendly models, as they aren’t brawling.
Two rival models 
are within reach: 
they are Brawling.
MOVING IN A BRAWL
Movement while in a Brawl is limited. When moving, Brawling 
models must finish any move within reach of all the rivals 
they were Brawling with at the start of their movement. If you 
want to move out of a brawl, you have to escape it.
ESCAPING A BRAWL
ESCAPING A BRAWL
To move out of a rival’s reach while Brawling, attempt a basic 
Move action, opposed by the Reflexes of one rival that is 
within reach of the moving model (opponent’s choice). If the 
action succeeds, move normally. If it fails, your action is used 
and the model doesn’t move. 
OUTNUMBERING ENEMIES IN BRAWLS
OUTNUMBERING ENEMIES IN BRAWLS
If the moving model has more friendly models within reach 
than rival models, they “outnumber” the enemy. Models may 
freely leave a Brawl when they outnumber their rivals.
The active Model 
(the Oyabun) 
attempts to drag 
the rival model 
within reach 
(Crusher). Her 
Reflexes roll is 
opposed by his 
Reflexes roll.
DRAGGING MODELS
A model may attempt to drag a model it’s brawling with, a lot like dragging an object. 
Dragging a friendly model that is not Brawling is opposed by the Obstacle die. 
Dragging a friendly Brawling model is opposed by the Reflexes of the Rival in the Brawl 
(opponent’s choice, if there’s multiple). 
Dragging a rival model you’re brawling with is opposed by that rival’s Reflexes. If the roll 
is successful, move your model and the target following the rules for dragging objects 
(page 20).
BRAWLING

SHOOTING INTO A BRAWL
A model that isn’t Brawling may attempt to attack a Brawling rival – but they can wound their own models. 
When you attack a Brawling rival who the active Model isn’t Brawling with, you count as targeting the 
selected rival and all models that are within reach of that target. The active Model’s roll is opposed by all the 
target models (friend and foe). 
Only the target model with the lowest defense roll (after modifiers) is affected by the action. In the case of a 
tie, the active Model’s rival chooses which model is affected (which may still roll high enough to resist).
NOTE: 
NOTE: Non-attack actions are resolved normally (they do not randomize their target).
BLASTS & TORRENT INTO A BRAWL
BLASTS & TORRENT INTO A BRAWL
Weapons that attack multiple models or affect an area are resolved normally when shooting into a brawl. 
For instance, when firing a blast or torrent weapon into a brawl, resolve the attack against the target(s) as 
you normally would for those weapons.

or interacting with the Battlespace does.
INSTALLING PROGRAMS
INSTALLING PROGRAMS
Netrunners can install a number of

your Netrunners have which programs 
installed. Programs have a rarity number, 
just like gear. You may not include more 
copies of a program than its rarity. A 
Netrunner may not install multiples of the 
same program.

be GREEN

 RED quality.

an Action token.
If a Netrunner fails to launch a program, 
they may not attempt to launch that 
program again during the same activation.
Program actions may not be used to take 
[RE]actions.

YELLOW, or RED

YELLOW,

 RED side face-up.

29
LOADED & RUNNING
LOADED PROGRAMS
LOADED PROGRAMS
The Loaded side will have an action the Netrunner 
can attempt (usually showing the Tech skill).
As with other actions, if the program’s action targets 
a rival, it is opposed by that rival’s skill. If it targets a 
friendly model, the program is opposed by the black 
Obstacle die.
RUNNING PROGRAMS
RUNNING PROGRAMS
Programs’ running sides list their ongoing effects. 
Many programs cause the Netrunner to be vulnerable 
while the program is running. 
The running side will also say when to reload the 
program, flipping it back to its loaded side. Many 
programs are reloaded when you Inspire Your Team.
A model may only launch its programs during 
its activation, and the program may not interrupt 
another action or be used to make [RE]actions.
COMBAT NETRUNNING
COMBAT NETRUNNING
Your Netrunners are your first line of defense 
against enemy hacking. When one of your models is 
targeted by a rival’s program, you may oppose their 
roll with a Netrunner instead of the declared target, 
if that Netrunner has a Path of Attack to the rival 
attempting to run the program.
If your Netrunner doesn’t stop the program, your 
Netrunner is now the target of the successfully run 
rival program.
HACKING YOUR RIVALS
HACKING YOUR RIVALS
Many programs have the effect “Hack target rival” 
when run. If these programs are run successfully, 
place a Hacked token on the target’s Character card. 
Characters may have more than one Hacked token at 
a time. 
Many programs affect “Hacked” 
rivals and have a greater effect on 
Characters with more than one 
Hacked token.
Because Gonks don’t merit 
decent cyberware, they can’t be 
Hacked. They can still be affected 
by programs that don’t involve 
Hacking tokens.
SAFEGUARDING YOUR BRAIN
SAFEGUARDING YOUR BRAIN
Cyberware has self-repair functionality built in: when 
you Inspire Your Team, remove up to one Hacked 
token from each of your Characters.
MEMORY LEAKS 
MEMORY LEAKS 
When a Netrunner Fumbles an attempt to run a 
program, they suffer a Stun 1 effect: they must 
immediately flip one ready Action token to used. 
If they have no ready actions, they take a wound 
instead (as per Stun). Either way, their activation 
then ends and control passes to their opponent.
Some programs’ abilities have “Dangerous” in their 
description. Like other Dangerous actions, Fumbling 
a Dangerous program causes a wound, in addition to 
the normal program Fumble Stun effect above.
All of a vulnerable model’s skills 
are reduced to zero, except 
the model’s Tech skill, which 
remains unchanged.

Token

## Page 30

RULE OF X
RULE OF X
Wherever a rule has an [X], the X will be replaced with a number on a Character or Gear 
card, such as “Pierce 2.”
ACCURATE
You may re-roll Accurate actions. You may only ever re-roll each die once.
ANIMAL
Animal models cannot take Ranged, Tech, Influence, or Medical actions.
ARMOR
Armor protects the wearer from ranged and melee attacks. Armor does not protect 
against other actions or attacks, such as a Netrunner using a Tech attack. A Character 
can have Armor in two ways, and they function just a little differently from each other. 
You might have Armor printed on a Character card, or you might have Armor printed 
on a Gear card.
GEAR CARDS WITH ARMOR
GEAR CARDS WITH ARMOR
Gear that has the Armor keyword can help protect 
a model, even soaking up a wound, but it will 
eventually be rendered unprotective.
A model with Armor gear may choose to add 
the Armor value from one piece of gear to their 
Opposed Roll, instead of their skill. You cannot 
combine Armor from multiple pieces of gear.
If the attack is successful, the model discards the 
Armor Gear card used, instead of taking a single 
wound (that wound is negated). 
If the attack fails, the Armor gear and the model  
are unaffected, as normal.
CHARACTER CARDS WITH ARMOR
CHARACTER CARDS WITH ARMOR
Some Models have an Armor value right on their 
card. 
Models with Armor ratings on their card can use 
their Armor against ranged and melee attacks, just 
like a Character with Armor gear. 
The difference is that the Armor on a model’s card 
isn’t removed if the attack is successful, and the 
wound isn’t negated. Instead, the model suffers a 
wound as normal. They can still use their built-in 
Armor value against later attacks. 
Armor which is on a model’s card may not be 
combined with Armor from that model’s gear. A 
model may only ever use one source of Armor 
against a single attack.
KEYWORDS & TERMS

BLAST
Blast actions affect a large area. When taking a 
Blast action, place an Impact token in base-to-base 
contact with the target, then roll to see if the action 
succeeds (and hits) or fails (and misses). This 
action is opposed by the target’s relevant skill as 
normal (often Reflexes against a Ranged attack).
When a Blast action succeeds, the targeted model 
(touching the Impact token) is affected by the action. 
If the action was an attack, they suffer a wound as 
normal. Then, resolve the Blast.
If the Blast action fails, the target is not affected by 
the initial action and the target model’s player may 
move the Impact token up to RED 
 RED away from its 
original location. Then, resolve the Blast. 
RESOLVING THE BLAST
RESOLVING THE BLAST
After a Blast action has placed its Impact token, 
the models within RED 
 RED of the Impact token will be 
affected by the action (including the original target), 
unless they can move out of the Blast area or resist 
the Blast’s effect. 
All models within RED 
 RED of the Impact token that have 
a ready Action token may take a basic Move Action. 
First the active player’s models may move (or at 
least attempt to move), then the rival’s models do 
the same.
Models not able to move farther than RED 
 RED from 
the impact must attempt a roll against the action’s 
original skill roll. All models that fail this roll suffer 
the Blast’s effect (often a wound). This could result 
in the primary target being affected twice (often 
resulting in two wounds). If ya didn’t already know 
this, it’s a bad day when you’re in contact with a thing 
that up and goes kabloomy.
FUMBLING BLAST ACTIONS
FUMBLING BLAST ACTIONS
If you Fumble a Blast action, the targeted rival places 
the Impact token in base contact with the attacker 
and the Blast is resolved in the same way as a “miss”, 
against the Obstacle die (instead of the original 
result). If the weapon is also Dangerous, the Blast will 
cause an automatic wound to the attacker, too.
BLASTS AND BARRIERS
BLASTS AND BARRIERS
If there is a barrier between a model and the center 
of the Blast, that model is not affected.
VERTICAL BLASTS 
VERTICAL BLASTS 
Blasts have a radius in all directions – including 
vertically. Models above and below the attack may 
be caught in the Blast too! 
The Crusher is far 
enough from the 
blast to be safe.
The Kyodai and the 
Ripper are within RED 
 RED 
of the impact token, 
and must now dive 
away or risk getting 
hurt by the blast.
The Oyabun is safe from this blast, 
because a barrier is in the way, but 
the Kyodai is in danger.

BULKY
Each Character may only equip one Gear card 
with the Bulky keyword.
COMPLEX
Complex actions may not be attempted if there 
are enemy models within RED
RED.
CRIT EFFECTS
Some special rules occur when a Crit is rolled 
and succeeds. On gear, these appear as (Special 
Rule) Crits. For example, “Deadly Crits.”
DANGEROUS
If a model rolls a Fumble when attempting a 
Dangerous action, they take a wound (and the 
action fails, as normal).
DEADLY
Deadly attacks deal an additional wound when 
they hit. Deadly is often triggered by a Crit, as in 
“Deadly Crits.” 
DEFENSE: [SKILL X]
When using the listed skill for an Opposed 
Roll, add X to your total. Note that unlike most 
effect keywords, this applies when the model is 
opposing an action.
DEPLOY
When you are directed to deploy a model during 
a game, take it and place it onto the Battlespace 
as directed by the effect. You do not need to 
spend EBs on the model, and it may act normally 
from that point on. This is most common with 
programs that deploy drones, but other models 
are sometimes deployed during a game as well.
DIFFICULT
Difficult actions can be used once and can only be 
used again after you Inspire Your Team.
When you use a Difficult action, place a used Action 
token on it as a reminder that it cannot be used. 
When you Inspire Your Team, remove the Action 
token to show that it is available again.
DISCARD
If you use an action on a Gear or Loot card 
with Discard, you must discard that card after 
resolving the action (success or failure). If the 
gear is printed on a Character’s card, use a used 
RED 
RED token to mark that it was already spent. 
DRONES
Drones are models that do not have their own 
Character or Gonk card. 
Drones are brought into play with a Program 
card. The Program card will specify what the 
drone may do and how. Once a drone is in play, 
it continues to act as outlined on the program’s 
Running side, regardless of whether the program 
is refreshed.
ATTACKING DRONES
ATTACKING DRONES
Drones are immune to actions other than attacks. 
When a drone is targeted by an attack, it defends 
using the Obstacle die. Its roll is unmodified and 
may not be re-rolled (even by Luck tokens).
If a drone suffers a wound, it is taken out but 
does not drop a body token.
EASY
Easy actions do not require a skill roll (an Action 
token is still used).
EXHAUST
When directed to “exhaust” a card, flip the card over 
to its back. When you Inspire your team, you refresh 
(flip face-up) any exhausted cards.
HEAL [X]
This action can restore wounds (page 16)
INDIRECT
Obstacles in this action’s Path of Attack have no 
effect: they do not add to the Opposed Roll. Note 
that barriers still have the normal effect.
PIERCE [X]
Pierce attacks reduce the value of the target’s 
Armor by X. If that takes it to zero or below, that 
Armor may not be used to oppose the attack.
PUSH
A successful Push action moves the target 
model RED 
 RED directly away from the active Model.

RAPID [X]
Rapid actions allow you to take the same action 
X times without using additional actions. 
A Rapid action can target the same model or 
target a number of different models. Resolve 
each of the Rapid actions separately, then 
resolve any and all [RE]actions in the order 
wounds were dealt, if any.
WALKING FIRE: SWITCHING TARGETS
WALKING FIRE: SWITCHING TARGETS
Rapid actions may switch targets between the 
repeated actions. This is called “walking fire.” 
Each new target must be within RED 
 RED of the 
previous target. For each target beyond the first, 
the action suffers a -1 penalty to the skill roll.
REFRESH
Refresh actions flip [X] Action tokens to ready.
RESERVE
If a model has rules that allow them to be in 
reserve, that means that you do not deploy them 
during setup. Instead, you’ll wait and deploy 
them into the Battlespace later in the game: set 
them aside for now.
DEPLOYING FROM RESERVES
DEPLOYING FROM RESERVES
Some scenarios have special rules about where 
reserve models can be deployed. If the scenario 
doesn’t specify reserve deployment rules, a 
model in reserve is deployed the first time they 
are activated: deploy them within reach of any 
board edge. When deployed, they must be either 
more than GREEN 
 GREEN away from all rivals OR within 
RED 
RED of a friendly model. The first time you 
activate a model in reserve, you must deploy it 
before taking any actions.
ROBOT
Characters with the Robot keyword cannot take 
Tech, Influence, or Medical actions. Robots cannot 
benefit from Heal effects, but do benefit from 
Repair effects. Lastly, Robots cannot use Gear 
cards that do not also have the Robot keyword.
SILENT
Targets of Silent actions may only [RE]act with 
an attack if the Path of Attack to the attacker is 
not obscured (meaning no obstacles or barriers 
cross the Path of Attack). Other [RE]actions, 
such as movement, may still be taken.
STUN [X]
Successful Stun actions flip [X] of the target’s 
Action tokens to used, if possible. Which Action 
tokens are flipped are chosen by the target’s 
player.
If a Gonk is stunned, mark the Gonk model with 
a used Action token. Stunned Gonks cannot take 
any actions until after that player Inspires Their 
Team. Then, remove all used Action tokens from 
the stunned Gonks.
SUPPRESSION
Models may only [RE]act to Suppression attacks 
with a move action. 
TORRENT
Torrent actions target all models in a Path of 
Attack, both friend & rival. This Path of Attack 
may be placed freely so that it covers multiple 
models with its width. Torrent actions may have 
multiple targets and each target will oppose the 
action separately (see page 25).
UNWIELDY
Do not add the Model’s skill to Unwieldy actions.
The Crusher begins a Rapid 
(3) attack against the Onee-
San, then walks his fire to the 
Oyabun (-1), and then walks 
his last shot to the Kyodai (-2).

## Page 34

34
When hiring a team for a scenario, players 
must obey the following rules. Campaigns 
(page 36) and Tournaments (page 38) 
may alter these rules; which will be 
defined in those sections.
FACTIONS
Each faction has its own Characters, Gear, 
Program, and Objective cards.
All of the models in a team must have 
that faction’s keyword (or be Mercs).
In addition, all Gear and Program cards 
equipped by Characters (even Mercs) 
must be chosen from the team’s Faction 
Gear and Program cards.
FIXERS
FIXERS
Some organized events may have the 
players choose a Fixer instead of a 
faction. In this case, the Fixer serves 
as the team’s faction and may allow 
the player to hire a team and purchase 
gear/programs from multiple factions 
as if they were one.
EB LIMIT
Each team has a total EB limit when 
hiring their team for a scenario. All of the 
team’s Characters, Gear, and Programs 
must have a total combined value equal 
to or less than this EB limit. 
STREET CRED
A team’s Street Cred is calculated 
differently for campaigns, tournaments, 
and one-off games (as defined in those 
sections). A team may only purchase 
and equip Gear and Program cards that 
are equal to or less than the teams 
Street Cred rating. 
HIRING A TEAM

## Page 35

35
MODEL TYPES
Some model keywords will determine how many of a 
specific model may be hired, and who may hire them.
LEADERS
LEADERS
Each team must have exactly one Character with the 
Leader keyword. 
Your Leader is the only “unique” Character. In 
general, you can have as many of the other 
Characters as you wish, (unless they’re a Specialist).
VETERANS
VETERANS
Most Characters have two versions of their card, and 
some have three. A Character card that has one or 
more Street Cred stars is a Veteran. Models with two 
or more Street Cred stars, are still Veterans, but are 
sometimes called Elites for clarity.
SPECIALISTS
SPECIALISTS
Specialist are more rare than other models. A 
team may include only one of each Character with 
the specialist keyword. A team may hire multiple 
different specialists, but only one of each.
MERCS
MERCS
Models that have the Merc keyword may be hired 
regardless of faction. 
GONKS
GONKS
The maximum number of Gonks you may hire for a 
scenario is equal to the combined Influence skill of all 
hired Characters. You must pay EB for each Gonk. 
ROBOTS
ROBOTS
Robots cannot equip or use Gear that does not also 
have the Robot keyword.
 
GEAR
When you purchase gear for your team, it must be 
equipped to a Character. The gear will stay equipped 
to that Character for the scenario. 
Some gear has the “Cybergear” keyword. Any 
Characters with Cybergear equipped are referred to as 
“Cyber-Characters”.
CHARACTER GEAR LIMITS
CHARACTER GEAR LIMITS
There are two limits placed on what Gear a single 
Character may equip: 
•	 A Character may only equip a single Gear card with 
the Bulky keyword.
•	 A Character may not equip two or more Gear cards 
with the same name. 
RARITY
RARITY
Gear and programs have a Rarity value on each card. 
Your team may not include more copies of a card 
than that card’s Rarity.
UNIVERSAL GEAR
UNIVERSAL GEAR
Some Gear cards are marked as Universal rarity. 
These cards add their rarity to the rarity of faction 
Gear cards to determine how many a team may use. 
Example: John has access to 1 Heavy Pistols in the 
Edgerunner’s Gear deck, but would really like more. 
He has access to a Universal Gear deck, which 
includes Heavy Pistols with Rarity 2. He’s able to 
purchase those pistols as well, for a total of 3 Heavy 
Pistols to equip to three team members.

## Page 36

36
Campaigns are a series of games where 
players track their gang’s progress 
towards a goal. There are two broad types 
of campaign; personal and organized. 
Personal campaigns are tracked 
individually as you play against rivals. 
You earn Street Cred, track objectives, 
and work towards winning your faction’s 
Prove Your Worth mission. 
In an organized campaign, players 
track their campaign team. You will 
only play that team in assigned games 
against opponents who are in the same 
organized campaign.
HQ & SUPPLY
Each team will have two pools of cards 
called the Supply and the HQ. 
The Supply contains all cards that the 
team might gain access to, but that they 
have not yet earned. This includes veteran 
Character cards for models as well as 
gear and programs with a Street Cred 
requirement greater than your team’s 
Street Cred.
The team’s HQ contains the cards the 
team has earned through play. Each 
team’s HQ begins play with all the basic 
versions of model cards of your faction. 
The team’s HQ also contains all Gear 
and Programs of the team’s Street Cred 
(or lower). Lastly the team’s HQ contains 
any Objective cards that have been 
achieved.
A team may only hire models or purchase 
gear/programs that are in their HQ. 
MERCS
MERCS
A team may begin a campaign with up 
to two basic Mercs in their HQ. If a Merc 
has the team’s faction keyword (such as 
Edgerunners), it doesn’t count against 
this limit.
SPECIALISTS
SPECIALISTS
An HQ may include more than one of a 
Specialist, but only one of each may be 
hired for a scenario.
STREET CRED
A team’s Street Cred is calculated by 
adding together the total number of Street 
Cred stars on all cards in the team’s HQ 
(not their Supply), including any earned 
from achieved Objective cards that have 
bonus Street Cred stars. 
If an effect lowers your Street Cred, you do 
not remove cards from your HQ. However, 
you cannot purchase gear or programs in 
your HQ if their Street Cred requirement is 
higher than your current Street Cred.
OBJECTIVE CARDS
Each faction has a deck of Objective 
cards. Before the Setup of each game, 
randomly draw four Objective cards and 
then discard one. The three remaining 
Objectives can be completed during 
the game. Objectives are hidden until 
completed. 
CONTROLLING QUARTERS
CONTROLLING QUARTERS
You control a board quarter if there are 
more friendly models completely within 
that quarter than rival models completely 
within that quarter.
COMPLETED OBJECTIVES 
COMPLETED OBJECTIVES 
When you complete an Objective, place 
it in front of you and gain its benefit 
immediately. Some Objectives only help 
you while choosing your team (such as 
Cybergear or extra EBs), these Objectives 
will not affect you until following games. 
Completed Objectives are added to your 
HQ after the game. Many Objectives 
feature a star icon and increase your 
team’s Street Cred.
There are three kinds of bonuses for a 
completed Objective: ongoing, recycles, 
and Cybergear: 
ONGOING
ONGOING rewards grant their bonuses 
every game.
RECYCLE
RECYCLE rewards must be discarded for 
effect. When an Objective that recycles is 
discarded, put it back into your Objective 
deck. It may be drawn and achieved again. 
CYBERGEAR
CYBERGEAR may be equipped to any 
model that is eligible to equip Cybergear. 
It is equipped when hiring the team 
without cost or Street Cred requirement. 
CAMPAIGNS

## Page 37

37
PROMOTING MODELS
Veterans can be earned through play, promoting the 
old version between games. When a scenario directs 
you to promote a Character, replace the Character’s 
basic card with the Veteran card in the HQ (or the 
Elite if they are already a Veteran).
GETTING MORE MERCS
GETTING MORE MERCS
When you promote a model, you may instead add a 
Veteran Merc to your HQ.
MAJOR INJURY
After resolving the Campaign Fallout of a scenario, 
players will need to determine if any of their models 
succumbed to a Major Injury.
RECOVERING FROM THE SCENARIO
RECOVERING FROM THE SCENARIO
Each player need to roll a die for each of their 
Veteran Characters who were wounded or taken out 
during the scenario. This includes models that were 
just promoted as a result of this scenario. Basic 
Characters do not need to make this check (they are 
easily replaced).
Roll the color die that matches the best Action 
token the Veteran had remaining at the end of 
the game. Veterans who are taken out roll two 
RED
RED dice. If the model rolls a Fumble, they have 
suffered a major injury! 
A model that has suffered a major injury might not 
die, but they’re not fighting for a while. Remove their 
card from your HQ and replace them with the basic 
version. Mercs are removed from your HQ, you won’t 
be able to use them until you re-acquire them in 
place of a promotion (see above).
MEDICS
MEDICS
If a model on a team has a MED Skill of 1 or more, 
they may attempt to save any Veterans that suffered 
a major injury. They may make a MED Skill Roll vs. 
the Obstacle die. 
If successful, the injured veteran recovers from the 
major injury and are returned to the HQ.
INJURED LEADERS
INJURED LEADERS
If the team’s Leader suffers a major injury, it is 
handled differently. If a Veteran Leader suffers a 
major injury they must be replaced with that model’s 
basic card. Then, add the “Wounded Leader” Objective 
card to your HQ. 
This temporarily reduces your team’s Street Cred by 
one until you win a game. The Wounded Leader card is 
never shuffled into your Objective deck.
OPTIONAL RULE:  
THE NIGHT MARKETS
An organized campaign or personal campaign player 
might use the Night Markets optional rule. The Night 
Markets make all gear and programs available to 
all teams, regardless of faction. In addition, rarity 
does not limit how many of the same gear can be 
purchased. Instead, the team HQ is limited in the 
cards it contains.
Each player’s HQ begins with 10 Gear or Program 
cards of their choice with a 0 Street Cred 
requirement. These cards may be from any faction’s 
Gear deck (and may combine decks). When a team 
gains Street Cred, it does not automatically add new 
Gear and Program cards to the HQ.
After each game, players may add Gear and Program 
cards to their HQ, regardless of winning or losing. 
Each player may add a single card of Rarity 1 to their 
HQ, or two cards of Rarity 2 or more. These cards 
may be from any faction but must have a Street Cred 
requirement equal to or less than the team’s current 
Street Cred.

## Page 38

38
TOURNAMENTS / ONE-OFF
One-off games are not part of any 
ongoing campaign. These may include 
friendly pick-up games down at the 
local shop or games played during an 
organized tournament.
FIXED-LIST TOURNAMENTS
In a tournament, the players must 
submit their team list to an organizer. 
This team will be used during each 
game of the tournament. The model’s 
will not promote, suffer major injury, or 
otherwise be changed by the result of 
the individual games.
STREET CRED
STREET CRED
In a tournament, a team’s Street Cred is 
defined by the tournament’s EB limit. This 
value is 1 Street Cred for each full 25 EB 
limit, to a maximum of 6. 
VETERANS
VETERANS
In a tournament, Veterans are purchased 
using the team’s Street Cred. A player 
may include a number of Veterans whose 
combined Street Cred stars are equal to 
or less than the team’s Street Cred.
MERCS
MERCS
In a tournament, a team may include up to 
two Mercs from other factions. Mercs of 
the team’s faction do not count towards 
this limit.
OBJECTIVE CARDS
In a tournament, objectives are used 
during round assignments and to 
break ties, they do not affect the game 
or provide any rewards. Tournament 
scenarios have three shared objectives 
detailed on the scenario.
If a tournament uses scenarios without 
pre-set objectives, the organizer may 
require players to declare three Objective 
cards in advance. 
When submitting their fixed-list, each 
player must note three of their Faction 
Objective cards. These chosen cards will 
be used in every game. Any Objectives 
that are achieved during each round’s 
game will be noted when submitting 
round results. 
These Objectives are not hidden during 
the game (you must reveal them to your 
opponent).
ONE-OFF VS. CAMPAIGN
Sometimes a player may wish to play a 
one-off game against a player who is on a 
personal campaign. 
In these games, the one-off player 
should determine their Street Cred by 
the EB limit (as if it were a tournament) 
and may include Veterans and Mercs in 
the same way.
This will likely result in a miss-matching 
of Street Cred values and different 
Veteran and Merc availability between 
the two players. This is acceptable, but 
remember to apply Underdog’s Luck (see 
page 12).

ACCURATE –
ACCURATE – You may re-roll Accurate 
actions, after rolling. You may only ever 
re-roll each die once (page 30).
ACTION –
ACTION – A single activity performed 
by a single model. Actions can include 
movement, attacking, healing, hacking, 
etc (page 11).
ACTION DICE –
ACTION DICE – The GREEN
GREEN, YELLOW
YELLOW, 
and RED 
 RED dice, used when attempting an 
action (page 4).
ACTION TOKEN –
ACTION TOKEN – A marker used to 
indicate whether a Character has an 
action available as well as the color of die 
used to resolve that action (page 4).
ACTIVATE –
ACTIVATE – The act of selecting a model 
to become the “active Model” (page 10).
ACTING MODEL – 
ACTING MODEL – The model that is 
currently resolving an action (page 12).
ACTIVE MODEL – 
ACTIVE MODEL – The model that is 
currently chosen to take actions by the 
player who is taking a turn (page 10).
ARMOR –
ARMOR – Protective equipment that the 
target of a Melee or Ranged attack may 
use for an Opposed Roll instead of using 
a skill. Armor provided by a Character 
card can always be used, but Armor from 
a Gear card can only be used until it fails 
to protect the wearer (page 30).
ATTACK –
ATTACK – An action that will cause a 
wound to the target if it is successful. 
May only target rival models (page 11).
BARRIER – 
BARRIER – A piece of scenery that a 
model cannot move through, such as a 
solid wall (page 21).
BASIC ACTIONS –
BASIC ACTIONS – 6 standard actions that 
any model may take (one for each skill) 
(page 14).
BATTLESPACE –
BATTLESPACE – The area over which the 
action of the game takes place, which 
includes scenery and deployment areas 
for each team (page 7).
BLAST –
BLAST – An action that affects models 
within a radius (page 31).
BODY TOKEN –
BODY TOKEN – A marker used to 
indicate where a model was taken 
out. Characters can loot the Body for 
equipment (page 17).
BRAWL –
BRAWL – A situation where rival models 
are within reach of each other. Brawling 
models have limited action and targeting 
options (page 26).
BULKY –
BULKY – A model may have a maximum 
of one Bulky gear (page 32)
CAMPAIGN – 
CAMPAIGN – A series of linked scenarios 
(page 36).
CHARACTER –
CHARACTER – A capable member of 
a team, typically with multiple Action 
tokens and special abilities (page 36).
CHARACTER CARD –
CHARACTER CARD – A card that shows 
the game rules of a specific Character 
model (page 8).
CLIMB –
CLIMB – Moving up or down a vertical 
barrier (pages 23).
COMMAND ACTION –
COMMAND ACTION – An action that 
allows a Character to activate another 
model, outside of the normal exchange of 
control (page 19).
CONTROL –
CONTROL – The active player is said to 
have control. Only the player with control 
can activate a model (page 19).
COMPLEX ACTION –
COMPLEX ACTION – Complex actions 
may not be attempted if there are enemy 
models within RED 
 RED (page 32).
CRIT –
CRIT – A “critical hit;” the highest value on 
each die. Crits are usually an “automatic 
success” and may trigger additional 
effects (page 12).
CYBER-CHARACTER – 
CYBER-CHARACTER – A Character with 
Cybergear equipped (page 35).
CYCLE – 
CYCLE – The Driver of this Vehicle may 
choose to bend the Limiter when moving 
(instead of rotating) (page 48).
DANGEROUS ACTION –
DANGEROUS ACTION – If a model rolls 
a Fumble when attempting a Dangerous 
action, they take a wound (page 32).
DEADLY –
DEADLY – Deadly attacks deal an 
additional wound when they hit (page 32).
DEADLY CRITS –
DEADLY CRITS – Attacks which deal an 
additional wound when a Crit is rolled 
(page 32).
DEFENSE [SKILL X] –
DEFENSE [SKILL X] – A bonus to the 
listed skill that only applies when the 
model is opposing (defending against) an 
action (page 32).

DEPLOY – 
DEPLOY – To place a model on the table, to enter the 
Battlespace during a game (page 33).
DIFFICULT ACTION –
DIFFICULT ACTION – A particularly complicated 
action; a Character can only use each Difficult action 
once until you Inspire (page 32).
DISCARD –
DISCARD – After using an action with Discard, the 
gear or Loot card with the action is removed from 
play (page 32).
DRAG –
DRAG – Using a move action to relocate a rival or 
other object (pages 20).
DRONES –
DRONES – Models that do not have their own 
Character or Gonk card (page 32).
EASY ACTION – 
EASY ACTION – Easy actions are actions that do not 
require a roll (an Action token is still used) (page 32).
EB –
EB – “Eurobucks,” the currency of Combat Zone; 
used when creating your team (pages 34).
ENCLOSED –
ENCLOSED – Models in this Vehicle cannot target 
models outside the Vehicle or be targeted by models 
outside of the Vehicle. Models may still attack with 
Vehicle mounted Mods from the within the Vehicle 
(page 51).
ESCAPE –
ESCAPE – Using a move action to leave a Brawl. 
Uses Reflexes opposed by a rival’s Reflexes, unless 
you outnumber your rivals in the Brawl (page 26).
EXHAUST –
EXHAUST – To flip a card face down; exhausted 
cards effectively have no text and cannot use any 
abilities or effects until they are refreshed (page 32).
FACTION –
FACTION – One of the groups competing to control 
the Combat Zone. A team can only include members 
of a single faction (plus Mercs) (page 34).
FAIL – 
FAIL – To unsuccessfully execute an action by 
having a lower or equal total than the Opposed Roll 
(page 12).
FALL –
FALL – When a model involuntarily moves vertically 
down. This may cause it to suffer wounds (page 22).
FRIENDLY –
FRIENDLY – Your models are considered “friendly” to 
each other (page 5).
FREE / FOR FREE ACTIONS –
FREE / FOR FREE ACTIONS – An action that can be 
taken without using an Action token (page 11).
FUMBLE –
FUMBLE – The lowest value on each Action die. 
Fumbles are an “automatic failure” and may trigger 
additional effects (page 12).
GEAR –
GEAR – Equipment or weapons a model can use 
during the game. Gear can give a model additional 
actions and/or other abilities (pages 8).
GONK –
GONK – A novice member of a team, representing 
rank-and-file grunts or muscle (page 8).
GONK CARD –
GONK CARD – A card that shows the game stats and 
rules for a type of Gonk model (page 8).
HACKED –
HACKED – When a Netrunner gains access to a 
rival’s cyberware, they place a Hacked token on 
that target. A target with a Hacked token is more 
susceptible to some programs (page 28).
HEAL, HEALING –
HEAL, HEALING – The act of removing a wound from 
a model; when a wound is healed, one Action token 
is restored to its normal color (page 32).
HEAVY –
HEAVY – When this Vehicle rams another model, the 
rammed model suffers 2 wounds instead of 1 (page 
48).
IMPACT TOKEN – 
IMPACT TOKEN – A marker used to indicate the 
center point of a Blast (page 31).
INDIRECT – 
INDIRECT – An action that ignores obstacles in the 
Path of Attack (page 32).
INSPIRE, INSPIRE YOUR TEAM –
INSPIRE, INSPIRE YOUR TEAM – One of two options 
a player has when they gain control; allows you to 
activate each of your Gonks (Gonks may take one 
action when they activate) while your Characters 
refresh their Action tokens (page 10).
INSTALL –
INSTALL – To assign a program to a Netrunner. A 
Netrunner can only run programs they have installed 
(page 28).
JUMP –
JUMP – A model may attempt to jump across a gap 
between scenery up to RED 
 RED (page 22).
KEYWORD –
KEYWORD – A descriptive word displayed on some 
actions and Character cards that is referenced by 
other rules (pages 30).
LAUNCH (A PROGRAM) –
LAUNCH (A PROGRAM) – To use a program’s effect. 
Launching a program does not use an action. If it is 
successfully launched, flip it to its Running side and 
resolve its effects (page 28).
LEADER –
LEADER – The centerpiece of a team and often its 
most powerful member. Each team must have one 
and only one Leader (page 35).
LIMITER – 
LIMITER – The measuring device used in the game, 
divided into GREEN
GREEN, YELLOW
YELLOW, and RED 
 RED lengths; 
the width of the Limiter measures the within reach 
distance (page 5).

wounds and been knocked out of the combat. When 
a model is taken out, it is replaced with a Body token, 
which can be looted (page 16).
TARGET – 
TARGET – The intended recipient of an action’s 
effects (pages 12).
TEAM – 
TEAM – All of the models controlled by a single 
player (pages 34).
TORRENT – 
TORRENT – Torrent actions target all models in their 
Path of Attack, both friend and rival (page 33).
UNWIELDY –
UNWIELDY – Do not add the model’s skill to 
Unwieldy actions (page 33).
USED – 
USED – A “face down” Action token, which cannot be 
spent to take an action (page 11).
VISIBLE – 
VISIBLE – Something is visible if a Path of Attack can 
be drawn to it (regardless of range) without being 
fully blocked by a barrier (page 24).
VULNERABLE –
VULNERABLE – A model that is Vulnerable has all of 
their skills, except Tech, reduced to 0 (page 29).
WALKING FIRE – 
WALKING FIRE – A penalty to Rapid actions when 
targeting multiple models (page 33).
WHEELMAN – 
WHEELMAN – An Expert Driver who can move 
Vehicles as nimbly as other Drivers handle Cycles 
(page 48).
WOUND, WOUNDED –
WOUND, WOUNDED – Primarily the effect of a 
successful attack, a wounded Character must 
replace one of their Action tokens with a RED 
 RED token. 
If they cannot, they are eliminated. A single wound 
will eliminate a Gonk (page 16).
BETA / NOT FINAL
BETA / NOT FINAL

BETA / NOT FINAL
