## Page 44

44
A Vehicle is a special type of model that requires a Driver. Vehicles don’t activate on 
their own. 
A Vehicle might allow Passengers to take special actions, but it does not activate or 
take actions on its own. Vehicle models are not Characters. 
VEHICLE MODEL “BASE” 
Many Vehicle models do not have a base, and instead the figure itself determines the 
area taken up on the Battlespace by the Vehicle. Treat the figure’s top-down footprint 
as the base of the model. If a Vehicle model has a base (such as a cycle), the model’s 
base is used as normal. Other models can move over a Vehicle as if it were an 
Obstacle/Barrier (depending on its height). Models may also stop on top of non-Bike 
Vehicles (see Holdin’ On page 49).
VEHICLE MODEL FACING
Unlike Characters and Gonks, Vehicle models have a facing. The front of the Vehicle 
model determines the front of the Vehicle facing. When a Vehicle moves, facing is 
important (see Piloting Vehicles on page 47). 
BUYING VEHICLES
A Vehicle can be included in a team like any other model by paying EB from your 
team’s budget. Vehicles do not have a faction and can be taken by any team. 
VEHICLE MODS
VEHICLE MODS
Vehicles are not Characters, therefore they cannot equip normal Gear cards. Instead, 
Vehicles have access to Mods. Some Mods have a keyword, such as Pintle or Turret. 
These Mods must be attached to a Mod slot on the Vehicle with the same Keyword 
(as noted on the Vehicle’s card). Only one Mod can be attached to each Keyword slot. 
Vehicle mods are universal and may be purchased by any team. Some Mods note a 
Vehicle type (like Car, Cycle, or Aerodyne); only that type of Vehicle can equip the Mod.
DEPLOYING VEHICLES
Vehicle models are placed during setup like any other model. In addition, other models 
may begin the game embarked in a Vehicle (see Passengers on page 46). 
When the Vehicle and other models are placed in reserve, declare which models are 
embarked in the Vehicle and place them on its card.
When a Vehicle arrives from reserve, if the scenario allows the model to deploy in 
contact with a board edge, the Vehicle must be deployed in contact with a board edge. 
If the scenario doesn’t allow for reserves to deploy along the board edge, follow the 
scenario guidelines (this may lead to narratively odd situations).
VEHICLE INTERACTIONS
Vehicles may be attacked and targeted by other actions, with a few exceptions. 
•	 A Vehicle model may not be the target of Influence actions.
•	 Vehicles may not be given free actions (including movement) by effects that do not 
specifically declare that they affect Vehicle models. 
•	 Since a Vehicle cannot be activated, it may not be targeted by Command effects, even 
those that aren’t Influence based.
Note that a Vehicle is rarely the acting or active model. If a Vehicle takes an action by 
itself (such as through a mod) or is forced to take an action by another model, then the 
Vehicle is the acting model during that action. 
VEHICLES

PASSENGERS
Every Vehicle has a number of seats on its card to 
place models on, one for the Driver and additional 
seats for Passengers. A Driver also counts as a 
Passenger when the rules affect Vehicle Passengers. 
EMBARK:
EMBARK: When a Character or Gonk’s movement 
ends with the model within reach of any Vehicle, the 
model may enter the Vehicle. The model is removed 
from the board and placed on the Vehicle card in an 
empty seat. If there are no empty seats, the model 
may not embark into the Vehicle. A model may not 
embark during the same activation they disembark. 
DISEMBARK: 
DISEMBARK: When an embarked model takes a 
move action, they may exit the Vehicle and be placed 
in base contact with the Vehicle before moving. 
The model may then take its movement from the 
disembarked position. A model may not disembark 
during the same activation they embark.
EVERYONE OUT!: 
EVERYONE OUT!: At the end of a Driver’s activation, 
all models embarked in the Driver’s Vehicle may 
choose to disembark (this is not an action). They do 
not move after deploying in this way.
SWITCH SEATS: 
SWITCH SEATS: A model in a Vehicle may take an 
Easy Reflexes action to move to another empty seat 
in a Vehicle they are a Passenger in. 
GUNNERS
GUNNERS
A model in a gunner seat may fire weapons in the 
linked mod slot if the Character begins its activation 
in that gunner seat. 
ATTACKING & DAMAGING VEHICLES
Vehicles have an Armor value and a number of Hull 
tokens that are only used to oppose attacks and 
track damage (not take actions). 
Vehicles cannot be healed by actions with the Heal 
keyword (they require the Repair keyword). 
Vehicles are taken out when they resist an attack 
with a RED 
 RED Hull token and fail. When a Vehicle is 
taken out, all embarked models are placed in contact 
with the Vehicle model before it is removed. 
BRAWLING
BRAWLING
Vehicles are never part of a Brawl and are not 
considered a Brawling model. They may be targeted 
by attacks as normal, even when rival models are 
within reach.
PASSENGER ACTIONS
Passengers may take actions from within a 
Vehicle. The Path of Attack is drawn from 
anywhere on the Vehicle’s base (the Passenger’s 
seat position does not matter). If the action targets 
another Passenger in the same Vehicle, the acting 
model and the target model are assumed to be 
within reach of one another.
ATTACKING PASSENGERS
Vehicle Passengers may be targeted by attacks. Path 
of Attack is drawn from the attacking model’s base 
to the Vehicle’s base when attacking Passengers, 
regardless of the target model’s seat position. 
A Passenger may choose to use the Vehicle’s Armor 
value (if any) when defending against attacks. 
BLAST & TORRENT
BLAST & TORRENT
Attacks with the Blast or Torrent keyword cannot 
target the models embarked in a Vehicle. These 
attacks must be directed at the Vehicle itself. 
Passengers (even those on cycles) are protected 
from the attack. If the Blast or Torrent attack takes 
out the Vehicle, every model embarked in the Vehicle 
suffers 1 wound. Models embarked in the Vehicle 
may not [RE]act to the Blast marker’s placement.

SPECIAL RULES
CYCLES 
Cycles are small two-wheeled Vehicles 
that are more nimble than other cars on 
the road. 
The Driver of a cycle may choose to bend 
the Limiter to measure movement rather 
than rotating normally (as per the color 
of the Steer action). The Vehicle must 
still move forward, but it moves along the 
curved Limiter path, rotating the Vehicle 
with the line’s curve. It does not otherwise 
rotate when moving this way. 
The Cycle must be able to move through 
the areas as normal, making Reflexes 
checks to avoid obstacles and hazards. 
WHEELMAN
A Wheelman is an expert Driver, either 
through experience, cyber, or both.
A Wheelman may move any Vehicle as if 
it were a Cycle when moving (by bending 
the limiter, as per a Cycle). 
In addition, if a Wheelman is a Vehicle’s 
Driver, the Wheelman’s Reflexes skill may 
be added when rolling to oppose Ranged 
attacks instead of the Vehicle’s Armor 
value.
RAMMING
When a Vehicle’s movement or rotation 
brings it into contact with another model, 
that model runs the risk of being rammed. 
When a Vehicle rams a model, the rammed 
model suffers 1 wound. If the ramming 
Vehicle has the Heavy keyword, the 
rammed model suffers 2 wounds instead. 
Characters may [RE]act when rammed 
by a Vehicle. If they are able to move out 
of the path of the Vehicle, or their [RE]
action takes out the ramming Vehicle, the 
Character suffers no wounds. Gonks and 
other Vehicles cannot [RE]act to the ram.
When a Vehicle rams another Vehicle 
or a barrier, the moving Vehicle stops, 
suffers 1 damage, and may not rotate at 
the end of the movement. When a Vehicle 
rams a non-Vehicle model, the Vehicles 
movement continues if that movement 
would bring it past the rammed model (it 
may not stop on top of another model). 
If the Vehicle cannot move fully over the 
rammed model, its movement stops at 
the rammed model’s base.
The Cycle is 
able to bend the 
Limiter and move 
along that path, 
maneuvering 
between Obstacles.

by a Wheelman 
and can also bend

BACKING UP
A Vehicle may move directly backwards during a 
Steer action, but the Vehicle moves RED 
 RED distance 
instead of GREEN
GREEN. The Vehicle still rotates based on

HOLDIN’ ON
Normally, models cannot end movement overlapping 
the base of another model. An exception to this rule 
are Vehicles. Models may move over Vehicles as if 
they were Barriers/Obstacles (as per their height), 
and may end their movement atop non-Bike Vehicles 
(the model must be able to physically stand in the 
location). If a Vehicle moves, every model atop 
the Vehicle must make a Reflexes roll against the 
Obstacle die. If they succeed, they resolve one of the 
following two options. If they fail, their rival resolves 
one of the following two options:
•	 Place the model in base contact with the moving 
Vehicle before it moves (if this is in front of the 
Vehicle, they are immediately rammed).
•	 Place the model atop the Vehicle after it moves (in 
a position it can physically stand).
Models atop a Vehicle are not Passengers, but are 
treated as Passengers when the Vehicle is taken out.

AERODYNES
From military VTOL to VIP commuters, aerodynes can be seen throughout the skies of 
Night City. In Cyberpunk RED: Combat Zone, we use these aerodyne rules to cover the 
vertical possibilities inherent in Vehicles that operate at various altitudes and hover. 
ALTITUDES
ALTITUDES
There are two altitudes at which an aerodyne operates. Battle, & Overwatch. Each 
of these altitudes has its own special rules for governing the aerodyne’s movement, 
attacks, and other interactions. 
BATTLE ALTITUDE
The lowest altitude is Battle. When an aerodyne is at this altitude it operates like 
any other Vehicle, except that it hovers above the ground. When an aerodyne’s pilot 
finishes a Steer action, it sets its hover height at RED
RED, YELLOW
YELLOW, or GREEN
GREEN. Place 
an Action token on the model’s base to signify its hover height. An aerodyne’s hover 
height may not be set lower than the height of terrain it is on (if the model is atop a 
YELLOW 
YELLOW tall building, the hover height may not be set to RED
RED). 
BATTLE ALTITUDE ACTIONS
BATTLE ALTITUDE ACTIONS
When at Battle altitude, the aerodyne obeys these special rules:
•	 The Path of Attack to and from the aerodyne treats all barriers of the current hover 
height or less as obstacles instead. For instance, an aerodyne hovering YELLOW
YELLOW 
treats barriers YELLOW
YELLOW and shorter as obstacles.
•	 When moving, aerodynes ignore any obstacle or barrier of GREEN
GREEN or lower height. 
They can climb up to GREEN
GREEN while moving without a Reflexes roll, and they do not 
suffer falling damage. 
•	 Aerodynes may move in any direction, rather than only into their forward facing.

## Page 51

51
OVERWATCH ALTITUDE
At this altitude, the aerodyne is above the 
Battlespace. Due to its elevation, only effectively 
enclosed buildings, such as those with a roof or 
similar structure, impede attacks.
When an aerodyne is at this altitude place the 
model off the board in contact with a board edge. 
We keep the aerodyne near the Battlespace to 
maintain the model’s visual presence. 
The physical position isn’t important to gameplay, 
so feel free to move the Vehicle to any edge. 
Passengers cannot disembark at this altitude.
OVERWATCH ALTITUDE ATTACKS
OVERWATCH ALTITUDE ATTACKS
The Vehicle is attacking from directly above the 
Battlespace, so only obstacles and barriers that 
provide cover from above will affect attacks from 
the Vehicle. In addition, it is too far from the 
Battlespace for actions at any range other than 
Long to be affective.
Remember to DISCUSS SCENERY WITH YOUR 
DISCUSS SCENERY WITH YOUR 
OPPONENT BEFORE THE GAME
OPPONENT BEFORE THE GAME. An enclosed area 
with protection from above (like an intact building)
can act as a barrier to actions at this altitude. 
However, the sheer advantage of such a high 
position allows actions to and from the aerodyne to 
ignore obstacles and barriers that aren’t enclosed.
The aerodyne and its Passengers may only target 
models in the Battlespace using Long range actions. 
They are too far away for anything else.
Likewise, actions may only target models at 
Overwatch altitude using Long range actions, even if 
both models are at Overwatch altitude.
If an aerodyne at Overwatch altitude is taken out, all 
Passengers are also taken out.
CHANGING ALTITUDE 
An aerodyne’s pilot may elevate or lower the 
Vehicle one step with a Steer action (they make no 
other movement).
When an aerodyne rises from Battle altitude to 
Overwatch altitude, set it off the board along a 
board edge.
When an aerodyne lowers from Overwatch altitude 
to Battle altitude, deploy it anywhere in the 
Battlespace.
When an aerodyne rises from Overwatch altitude to 
higher altitude, remove it from the Battlespace and 
set it in reserve. 
EMBARKING/DISEMBARKING
When a model disembarks an Aerodyne, they risk 
falling. When a model disembarks an Aerodyne, the 
model is assumed to fall from the Aerodyne’s hover 
height to the surface below. 
For instance, if an Aerodyne is at YELLOW 
 YELLOW height, 
then the disembarking model would fall YELLOW 
 YELLOW to 
the street below. However, if the Aerodyne is above 
raised terrain (like the roof of a building) the distance 
fallen is reduced, as the fall is from YELLOW 
 YELLOW height 
to the roof.
When a model on the Battlespace floor wishes to 
Embark an Aerodyne, the Aerodyne must be at RED 
 RED 
hover height. To Embark from a raised position, the 
Aerodyne must be within the same height band as 
the surface where the model is standing. 
For instance, to Embark from a roof that is YELLOW 
 YELLOW 
high, the Aerodyne must also be at YELLOW 
 YELLOW hover 
height. When measuring to the Aerodyne’s base to 
determine if the model is within range to Embark, 
ignore verticality (measure top-down), as the base is 
not representative of the model’s height.

## Page 52

52
COMPETITIVE SCENARIOS
Don’t let the name fool you. Competitive 
scenarios aren’t just for tournaments or the 
most hardcore of gamers. These scenarios 
are designed to put the players on an 
even field, which makes them perfect for 
players who are new to the game, looking 
to test a team, or who are honing their 
tactical skills. These scenarios are also very 
useful to tournament organizers, as they 
present a variety of options well suited to a 
tournament environment.
WHAT MAKES A  
SCENARIO COMPETITIVE?
There are two main factors that make a 
scenario competitive in nature; identical 
deployments and a shared objective. In 
addition, no player has sole access to 
resources or actions (from the scenario) 
that the other player doesn’t also have.
Identical deployment areas ensure that no 
player has a larger foothold on the board 
than the other. In casual play, this may 
still lead to slight imbalances of starting 
position, as the scenery is unlikely to be 
symmetrical. This is part of the challenge, 
and many scenarios account for this 
possibility in their design. However, some 
tournament organizers prefer to use 
symmetrical scenery. Rest assured, this 
won’t break the game either, so set up your 
tables as you wish!
Shared objectives also make a scenario 
competitive. This means that the players are 
both attempting to achieve the same goal. 
Either killing a certain number of enemies, 
grabbing Objective markers, hoarding 
Loot cards, or any number of possible win 
conditions. Scenarios where the players 
have different goals (like one stealing, the 
other protecting) are Narrative scenarios.
ANATOMY OF A SCENARIO
The scenarios in this book have been 
standardized and revised. They include the 
following sections:
Map: This map depicts a standard 22”x30” 
game board. In larger games, the deployment 
zones and Objectives are likely to be further 
apart than depicted. This is intended, and 
these scenarios all support play on a variety 
of table sizes.
Number of Players: This is the number of 
players the scenario was designed for. 
Initiative: Before setup, the players roll for 
initiative. Each player rolls the shown die 
and adds the noted skill bonus from their 
Leader. Players re-roll ties, and treat Fumbles 
and Crits as their value (1 and die maximum, 
respectively). The player with the higher result 
is the player “with initiative”. This will be used 
to determine various roles in the scenario 
(like attacker and defender).
Complexity: Scenario complexity is rated in 
three ascending levels; Minimal, Simple, and 
Expert. Playtime is typically based on the EB 
total players select, and the complexity of the 
scenario. Minimal complexity scenarios play 
the fastest, and are designed to get models 
on the board and players rolling dice as 
quickly as possible.
Setup: This section defines the setup for the 
scenarios. This determines how the players 
deploy both their models and any Objective 
markers, and who goes first.
Rules Text: Any special rules for the scenario 
are listed here with their own heading.
Special Actions: Some scenarios have special 
actions. The rules text will explain who can 
take the action, and under what conditions.
The Win: This is how the players win the 
game. It might also include the trigger that 
ends the game.
Campaign Fallout: This determines what 
happens if the players are engaged in a 
campaign game.
Competitive
HALF OF STARTING MODELS
Some scenarios have win conditions like "when a player has 
taken out half or more of their rival's starting models". This 
number is based on the number of models on the team at the 
start of the game. Models generated during the game do not 
raise this starting value, but they do contribute normally to 
victory if taken out.

on the order it was removed.

DON’T FENCE ME IN
SCENERY CONSIDERATIONS
This scenario was designed to play using 
Monster Fight Club’s Chain Link Fence 
scenery. Similar fence models will work fine, 
you need ten 4-inch (ish) fence sections to 
split between the players.
THE SETUP
The player with initiative is the Shocker, the 
other player is the Tazer. 
First, the Shocker chooses a board quarter 
and places their team within RED 
 RED of that 
board quarter’s edges. They may hold models 
in reserve.
The Tazer places their team within RED 
 RED of 
the edges of the opposite board quarter. They 
may hold models in reserve.
Each player takes 5 sections of fence. 
Starting with the Shocker, the players take 
turns placing a single section of fence 
anywhere on the Battlespace. Note that 
connecting multiple fences increases their 
strength (see Electric Fences), regardless of 
who placed which sections of fence.
The Shocker begins with control.
ELECTRIC FENCES
Models may take the De-Activate action.
De-Activate actions and Reflexes check made 
to parkour/climb/traverse any fence section 
are Dangerous. 
In addition, if three or more fence sections 
are connected (in an unbroken chain) then 
models suffer 2 wounds from the Dangerous 
effect instead of 1. 
If five or more fence sections are connected, 
then the model is taken out by the Dangerous 
effect, instead of suffering wounds.
THE COOL
Whenever a model triggers the Dangerous 
effect of an Electric Fence, either player 
may spend a Luck token to force another 
model within YELLOW 
 YELLOW to roll Reflexes vs the 
Obstacle die or suffer the same effect. 
THE WIN
A player wins when they take a fifth Fence 
section via the De-Activate action. If every 
model is taken out before this can be done, 
the Tazer player wins.
CAMPAIGN FALLOUT
The winner may promote a surviving Character of their choice.
Competitive
DE-ACTIVATE
[Dangerous] Remove a single Fence section within reach and set it aside as your score. The 
Dangerous damage from this action is increased based on the number of connected fences 
(see Electric Fences).
COMPLEXITY: EXPERT
INITIATIVE:
NUMBER OF PLAYERS: 2
+

the fall damage.

 RED above street level is worth 1

 YELLOW above street level is 
worth 2 Graffiti points.

 GREEN above street level is 
worth 3 Graffiti points.

 GREEN above street 
level is worth 4 Graffiti points.

and places their team and the Satellite within

the Transmitter within RED 
 RED of the opposite 
board edge. They may hold models in reserve.

of the four remaining Cyber City scenery 
pieces (Dispenser or Terminal) and placing it 
anywhere in a board quarter that doesn't have 
a Dispenser or Terminal, and at least RED 
 RED 
from any other Cyber City piece.

reach of a Cyber-City scenery piece, it may 
attempt a free Tech roll against the Obstacle 
die. If successful, resolve the following effect:

ready Action token, it activates next.

## Page 86

86
NARRATIVE SCENARIOS
These scenarios put a focus on the story of 
the scenario over balance considerations. 
While these scenarios can certainly be won 
and lost by either player, they don’t start the 
players off in symmetric positions. 
These scenarios see the players taking on 
drastically different roles in a variety of 
situations.
HIRING YOUR TEAM
While a competitive scenarios are designed 
to work with either pre-chosen or scenario-
chosen lists, narrative scenarios are designed 
for players to tailor their team beforehand. 
When playing a narrative scenario, players 
should use scenario-chosen teams. This 
means that the player hires their team after 
they know what scenario they are playing. 
Although, the players probably don’t know 
what side of the scenario they’ll be on!
In some cases, players may wish to choose 
their leader, then roll for initiative, and then 
hire the rest of their teams. This is particularly 
useful when building a narrative.
CUSTOMIZING
These scenarios aren’t intended to be the only 
way to play! These narrative scenarios make 
a great starting place for creating your own 
stories. In organized campaigns or events, 
the organizer might want to assign narrative 
missions to particular players, changing up a 
few details to bring the scenario in line with 
the story. Some easy changes include:
•Large board: Play the scenario with more 
space to maneuver.
•Reserves: Place part of one or both teams in 
reserve.
•Objectives: Play with more or fewer 
Objectives.
•Added Rules: Some scenarios have rules that 
work anywhere, like Pivot Point, which can be 
added to other scenarios.
•Removed Rules: Some scenario rules, like 
The Cool, can be safely removed to make a 
scenario more streamlined.
•Cyberpsycho: Include one or more psychos.
Narrative

---

## Page 102

102
RACE THROUGH THE RUINS
VEHICLE MAYHEM
This scenario is best played on a larger 
Battlespace than normal, as it is designed for 
teams with Vehicles. In this scenario, players 
will use two 30” x 22” boards (or mats), leap-
frogging the rear board from the back to the 
front as the race progresses. 
THE SETUP
The player with initiative may choose to be 
the Attacker or Defender.
Place two empty 30” x 22” boards together 
with their short board edges matched, so that 
they form a 60” x 22” board. 
The Attacker choose a long board edge and 
deploys their Vehicle models within RED 
 RED 
of a short board edge within that quarter. 
Then, the Attacker deploys all of their team 
embarked in friendly Vehicles. 
The Defender deploys their Vehicle models 
within RED 
 RED of the edge of the remaining long 
board half. Then, the Defender deploys all of 
their team embarked in friendly Vehicles. 
Any models that cannot be deployed into 
Vehicles are held in reserve.
The opposite long board edge of the front 
board is the finish line. The Attacker has 
control first.
DISTRACTED DRIVING
Move and Steer actions are never Easy. This 
scenario represents Vehicles moving at high 
speeds over open terrain. The single Steer 
action represents additional maneuvers the 
Drivers take, in addition to ludicrous speeds.
ENDLESS ROAD
When a Vehicle touches the finish line, the 
Vehicle’s Driver’s player takes an Objective 
marker. Then, take the board without the 
finish line and move it in front of the current 
finish line. The long board edge on the moved 
board is now considered the current finish 
line. All models on the moved board go into 
reserve.
Vehicle models enter from reserve anywhere 
touching the non-finish line short board edge.
MODELS ON FOOT
Models on foot will be left in the dust. After 
a model activates, if it not in or on a Vehicle, 
remove it from play and place it in reserve.
THE WIN 
When the players have taken a combined 10 
Objective markers, the player that took the 
last marker wins.
CAMPAIGN FALLOUT
The winner may promote a surviving Character of their choice.
Narrative
COMPLEXITY: EXPERT
INITIATIVE:
NUMBER OF PLAYERS: 2
+
. . .
FINISH LINE
FINISH LINE

## Page 108

108
MULTI-RIVAL SCENARIOS
There’s no shortage of enemies in the Combat 
Zone, and when players gotta fight more than 
one crew, they can use multi-rival scenarios 
to play against more than one rival. Most of 
the multi-rival scenarios are for three players, 
so that groups with an odd number of players 
can still involve everyone in games. 
Multi-rival scenarios may be symmetric 
(perhaps all models are in reserve), they 
may put two players against one, or 
they could just be weird. While all the 
maps shown on these scenarios are 22”x30”, 
multi-rival games often benefit from playing 
on a 44”x30” Battlespace.
MULTI-RIVAL INITIATIVE
Like other scenarios, players will roll for 
initiative. If two or more players tie, all the 
tied players re-roll, and this continues until 
no players are tied. Players re-roll until there 
are no ties, so this can cause players to re-roll 
multiple times, or to make players re-roll when 
another player re-rolls into a tie with them. 
CONTROL
Control order is defined in each scenario’s 
Setup (near the end of the section). This 
order is circular, so players may wish to 
arrange themselves clockwise around the 
Battlespace to make play progression feel 
more natural.
When a player takes control out of sequence 
(usually with a Command action) the order 
continues from them, to the next person in 
the order. This means that players can work 
together to stall a rival’s victory by taking well 
timed Command action. While effective, this 
can be hard to maintain once their [RE]actions 
start flying!
Multi-Rival

## Page 118

118
 SOLO CAMPAIGNS
While organized campaigns are a lot of fun 
and a great way to build a community, solo 
campaigns allow players to progress their 
team and work towards becoming a legend 
of Night City on their own schedule. There are 
two types of campaigns, Starter campaigns 
and Legends campaigns, but both are played 
in very similar ways.
STARTER CAMPAIGN
This campaign is played with the contents of 
a faction starter box. For these campaigns 
the player has access to all of the gear in their 
Faction deck that has a Street Cred rating 
equal to your team’s total Street Cred (or 
lower). Players also use the Faction Objective 
deck to randomly draw bonus Objectives 
each game. As the team gains Street Cred it 
might add other Characters, such as Mercs, 
but the gear and Objectives are locked to your 
faction.
When playing a Solo campaign, the player 
follows these after games:
1.	 Play a game.
2.	 Resolve any Campaign Fallout.
3.	 Determine Major Wounds.
4.	 Calculate current Street Cred.
5.	 Adjust HQ with Faction cards of new 
Street Cred (if needed).
LEGENDS CAMPAIGN
In Legends campaigns, the player guides 
their team to 10 Street Cred, just like a 
Starter campaign, but the method for 
choosing teams and populating the HQ is 
very different.
STARTING A LEGENDS CAMPAIGN
To begin a Legends campaign, the player 
must first choose what cards will go 
into their HQ. A team has access to their 
Faction cards (both Gear and models), as 
well as any cards in the Universal Gear and 
Programs pool.
The player first populates their HQ with 10 
basic Character cards (no Veterans), 1 Gonk 
card, and 1 basic Leader card. Teams may 
not add Merc models from other factions at 
this time (but may later when promoting).
Once the player has chosen their Characters, 
they then choose 10 Gear and Program 
cards with 0 Street Cred requirement. These 
cards may come from the Faction Gear or 
Universal Gear. The team does not have 
access to its entire Faction deck at all times, 
they only have access to cards drafted 
during the campaign.
Example: John wishes to begin a new 
Legends campaign. He chooses to play 
Lawmen, with the Hard-Boiled Detective as 
his leader. He then selects 10 basic Lawmen 
Character cards to add to his HQ. Finally, he 
chooses Rookie as his Gonk card.
Next, he select 10 Gear and Program cards. 
He chooses to load up on 2 Airhypos and a 
Heavy Pistol from the Universal Gear, as well 
as some of the basic Lawmen tools from his 
Faction deck.
ORGANIZED CAMPAIGNS
Organized campaigns are a great way to build your local 
community. In an Organized campaign, the players play 
Legends solo campaigns, but only matchups played in the 
campaign count, and the Organizer will determine rivals. Many 
Organized campaigns play 1 or 2 scheduled games per week.
Solo Campaigns

---

## Page 119

119
PROMOTING CHARACTERS
After each scenario, the player may have the 
opportunity to promote. When you promote a model, 
you choose a surviving Character and exchange their 
Basic card in your HQ for the Veteran card. If they are 
already a Veteran, they can exchange their Veteran 
card for their Elite card (if the Character has one).
Specialists Notes: A player’s HQ may contain 
multiple copies of the same Specialist, at the same 
or varied promotion levels. However, this does not 
allow a player to field more than one of a specific 
Specialist (of any SC) in their team during a game. 
Instead, these Specialists are redundant, which can 
matter greatly if a Specialist suffers a major injury!
EXPANDING THE HQ
After every scenario (win or lose) the player may draft 
a new card into their HQ.
Each player may add one Program or Gear card to 
their HQ after each game. This card is chosen from 
their Faction deck or the Universal Gear pool.
The Gear card added to the HQ may be of any SC 
value. However, to purchase the gear to use in a 
game, your team must have a current total SC equal 
to or greater than the Gear card, so don't pickup too 
many toys your team can't use yet!
Alternatively, a player may choose to recruit a Basic 
Character. When a player recruits, they choose a 
basic Character or Gonk card to add to their HQ. The 
Character must be of the team's faction or a Merc. 
OBJECTIVE CARDS
Legends campaigns do not use Faction Objective 
decks. Instead, they have access to Universal 
Objective decks. These decks are theme based, 
rather than faction specific. These decks have been 
re-balanced, and they feature more effects that occur 
during games. Those effects that do affect later 
games are more limited than the Faction decks, with 
players gaining additional HQ options, but no direct 
power advantage.
If a player does not have a Universal Gear deck, or 
they don't wish to use one, they can choose to play 
the game without secondary Objectives. If they do so, 
they may draft an additional Gear card or one Basic 
Character of their faction if they win the scenario (but 
only if they win).
PROVE YOUR WORTH
The goal of a both campaign types is to earn 10 
total Street Cred (either from Veterans/Elites or from 
Faction Objectives). Once this is done, the player may 
begin challenging rivals to play the player's Prove Your 
Worth scenario. Each faction has a Prove Your Worth 
scenario, and if you can win your faction’s scenario, 
you win the campaign!
PROVE YOUR LEGEND
In addition to their faction's Prove Your Worth 
scenario, players in a Legends campaign may 
attempt other faction's Prove Your Worth scenarios. 
You must have 10 Street Cred to attempt these 
scenarios, and you must also meet an additional 
requirement (as noted on each scenario).
CHALLENGE RATINGS
Each Prove Your Worth scenario has a challenge rating 
to (roughly) explain it's relative difficulty. Different 
teams will naturally find some scenarios easier or 
harder to complete, but the default difficulty serves as 
a guide.
PRO
These scenarios are slanted against the proving team, 
but only slightly. You probably won't need any special 
planning to have a chance at victory.
TRICKY
While not impossible, you'll want to have the right gear 
and crew to succeed, and if your rival knows what 
they're doing, they can make things very tough on you!
HARD
These scenarios are slanted heavily against the proving 
team, and victory without the right team and gear might 
prove impossible.
EXTREME
These are the most difficult scenarios to prove 
yourself. They are slanted against the proving team to 
an unfair level. If you can win a campaign with one of 
these scenarios, you get to brag about.
Solo Campaigns

## Page 132

132
INVESTIGATION STORY ARC
The Investigator is attempting to solve a murder, 
and the Culprits would like to avoid various 
problems that would result. Over the course of 
these three scenarios, the Investigator player 
must gather clues in a hunt for the truth.
Each player will need this Investigation Sheet. The 
Investigator player will use this sheet to solve the 
mystery (marking off wrong answers until only 
the correct remains), while the Culprit player will 
use this sheet to secretly write down the solution 
in advance. 
The Culprit must choose an answer to the three 
questions of the Investigation from the presented 
answers. These answers are secret and should be 
hidden from the Investigator player until directed 
to reveal them by the scenarios. 
Each Scenario will have a special action that the 
Investigator can take that will have a chance to 
reveal a clue. When a clue is successfully revealed, 
the Culprit player must tell the Investigator 
player one of the incorrect answers to one of the 
questions (if any remain). The Investigator player 
can then cross the wrong answer off their list. As 
the campaign progresses the Investigator will be 
able to solve the crime (or get close) through the 
process of elimination. 
After all three scenarios, the Investigator player 
will attempt to answer all three questions 
correctly. If they correctly guess all three 
answers, they have successfully built a case 
against the Culprit. If they fail, the Culprit has 
gotten away with it!
HOW WAS THE VICTIM 
MURDERED?
•Shooting
•Poisoning
•Hacked cyber-organs
•Engineered Overdose
•Suffocation
WHO PAID FOR THE HIT? 
•Arasaka
•Militech
•Rival Gang Leader
•Criminal Associate
•Romantic Partner
WHY DID THEY WANT  
THE VICTIM DEAD?
•Pride. The victim gave personal insult to the 
wrong person.
•Theft. The victim was in possession of high 
value stolen property.
•Just Business. The victim stood in the way of 
financial concerns.
•Infidelity. Victim was involved in an affair.
•Mistaken identity. The victim was incorrectly 
identified as the real target.
Story Arc
STORY ARCS
Story Arcs are a series of three scenarios that follow a team 
as they attempt to achieve a goal. These 3-part arcs are 
designed to be played in sequence and against the same rival. 
You can play these stories as part of another campaign, but 
they should be played in order and against the same rival.

ENDING THE INVESTIGATION
Now it’s time to score the entire campaign 
and determine the winner. 
•The winner of Scenario 1 earns 2 points.
•The winner of Scenario 2 earns 3 points.
•The winner of Scenario 3 earns 4 points.
The Investigator makes their official guesses 
as to HOW, WHO, and WHY. If they are correct, 
they earn points. If they guess wrong, the 
Culprit earns the points.
•The HOW is worth 2 points.
•The WHO is worth 3 points.
•The WHY is worth 4 points.
The player with the most points has won the 
Story Arc. Was justice served? Or, did another 
crime go unsolved on the streets of Night City?
CAMPAIGN FALLOUT
The winner may promote a surviving Character of their choice.

follow your teams as they hunt for cutting-
edge chrome pulled from the corpses of

instructions for choosing teams. This 
storyline will refer to the two players as the

as you work towards your Prove Your Worth 
mission, just make sure these scenarios are 
played in the correct order, and against the

BLOCK BATTLE STORY ARC
Block Battle is a series of three scenarios 
that follow a gang as it defends itself from 
invasion. To win, you’ll seek victory over 
three scenarios, as your opponent assails the 
neighborhood you call home. 
PLAYING THIS CAMPAIGN
Each Scenario will give instructions for 
choosing teams. This storyline will refer to 
the two players as the Locals player and the 
Invader player.
This campaign is designed to be played in 
sequence and against the same rival. You can 
play this story as part of another campaign, 
but these scenarios should be played in order 
and against the same rival.
PREPARE THE BATTLEFIELD
This story happens in a single location. All 
three scenarios should be played on the 
same urban Battlespace with terrain in the 
same spot. Other environments can work, 
but the story’s mechanics will be using terms 
appropriate to a city. 
The players will define the four board edges 
by defining what that board edge represents.
Starting with the Invader, take turns assigning 
a definition to an unnamed board edge until 
all four edges are assigned a descriptor. 
Then, the Locals player places the terrain on 
the board. Each board quarter should have 
at least one building (or similarly terrain 
piece), and the Locals should be aware that 
cramming all the terrain into one quarter is 
not to their tactical advantage. 
Story Arc
STREET
Models within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this board edge 
gain +1 to their Tech skill.
ROAD
Models within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this board edge 
gain +1 to their Medical skill.
BOULEVARD
Models within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this board edge 
gain +1 to their Influence skill.
AVENUE
Models within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this board edge 
gain +1 to their Ranged skill.
WAY
Models within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this board edge 
gain +1 to their Melee skill.
COURT
Models within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this board edge 
gain +1 to their Reflexes skill.
BAY
Models may deploy from reserve anywhere 
within RED 
 RED of this edge.
BARRENS
When a model within YELLOW 
 YELLOW of this 
board edge Crits, gain a Luck token.

## Page 148

SOLO & CO-OP SCENARIOS
Solo & Co-Op play pits the players’ teams 
against Cyberpychos in a desperate bid for 
survival. Cyberpsycho models are Character 
models that have a Cyberpsychosis card. The 
Cyberpsychosis card determines their actions 
when a Cyberpsycho activates. 
ACTIVATING CYBERPSYCHOS
During Co-Op play, control passes from one 
player to the other. The players choose a fixed 
order to take control. 
At the end of each player’s control that player 
must resolve an enemy activation, before 
control passes to the next player. The still 
active player chooses a Cyberpsycho that 
has a ready Action token. That Cyberpsycho 
becomes the active model and activates.
When a Cyberpsycho activates, it spends 
Action tokens to take actions. It starts by 
spending the topmost ready token, and 
proceeds down. 
To resolve Cyberpsycho activation, follow 
these steps:
STEP 1: DETERMINE FOCUS
The Cyberpsycho’s focus is the visible model 
in the closest range band (Reach, RED
RED, 
YELLOW
YELLOW, GREEN
GREEN, then Long). 
If there are multiple models in the closest 
range band, the active player chooses which 
of those models is the focus. Models “on the 
line” between range bands are considered to 
be in the closer band.
If there are no visible models, the 
Cyberpsycho does not take any Actions and 
its activation ends.
When a model becomes the focus, they 
may take the Sneak action as a [RE]action, 
opposed by the Cyberpsycho’s Reflexes. 
Cyberpsycho’s always ignore other 
Cyberpsychos (and will not select each 
other as a Focus).
STEP 2: DETERMINE ACTION
The Cyberpsychosis card will define the 
action that the Cyberpsycho takes based on 
its range to the focus. 
STEP 3: RESOLVE ACTIONS
The Cyberpsycho spends its topmost ready 
Action token to take any actions required by 
the Cyberpsychosis card. If the action has a 
target, the focus is the target (such as with 
most attacks). 
Since the Cyberpsycho can't roll dice for 
itself, the players must do it. However, 
players may not spend their Luck tokens on 
the Cyberpsycho’s die rolls. 
STEP 4: REPEAT
Once the action is resolved, the Cyberpsycho 
will take another action if it still has unspent 
Action points. As before, it will spend the top 
most ready Action token.
It will not determine a new focus, 
instead taking the action noted on the 
Cyberpsychosis card based on the distance 
to the focus.
If the Cyberpsycho’s focus is taken out, 
succeeds at a Sneak [RE]Action, or otherwise 
stops being visible, the Cyberpsycho will 
immediately attempt to determine a new 
focus and continue taking actions.
Title
Reach 
Range
RED
RED 
Range
YELLOW
YELLOW 
Range
GREEN
GREEN 
Range
Long 
Range
Skill  
Bonus
SNEAK
Models may only take this [RE]action when they become a Cyberpsycho’s focus. If 
successful, the Cyberpsycho ignores the model and chooses another legal focus. This 
model cannot become the focus for the remainder of this Cyberpsycho activation.
Solo / Co-op

## Page 149

149
MOVEMENT
When a Cyberpsychosis card calls for a model to move, 
the Cyberpsycho moves in a straight line as described. 
This may take the Cyberpsycho over obstacles, but it 
will not climb unless required to reach its destination 
(such as when moving to a marker atop a building). If 
it can move around barriers it will do so by the shortest 
distance. The Cyberpsycho must make any Reflexes 
rolls required to traverse obstacles and terrain.
ATTACKS
Cyberpsychos typically make Basic attack actions, 
with bonus effects based on their Cyberpsychosis 
card. These attacks target the focus unless specifically 
noted otherwise. 
SPECIAL ABILITIES 
Characters with special abilities or actions typically 
won’t take those actions as a Cyberpsycho. However, 
some situational abilities may still apply, such as 
Defenses, or effects that grant conditional bonuses.
KEYWORDS
All Cyberpsychos gain the CyberCharacter keyword in 
addition to their other keywords.
CORNER CASES
When a Cyberpsycho’s actions interact with an odd 
board or become murky due to a quirk of positioning 
(or other interaction), the active player makes any 
determination required. 
CYBERPSYCHO DAMAGE
When a Cyberpsycho opposes an action it does 
so using the bottom most non-RED
RED Action token. 
Cyberpsycho’s never risk a RED 
 RED Action token unless 
they have no other available Action tokens.
CYBERPSYCHO [RE]ACTIONS
When a Cyberpsycho takes damage, it will [RE]act 
using the top most ready Action token. The model that 
damaged the Cyberpsycho becomes the focus for the 
[RE]action, and the range to the focus determines the 
action as per the Cyberpsychosis card. 
CYBERPSYCHO REFRESH
After any player Inspires Their Team, all Cyberpsycho 
models also refresh their Action tokens. When this 
happens, the player does not resolve a Cyberpsycho 
activation before passing control. Note, the 
Cyberpsychos refresh their Action tokens after 
the player fully resolves Inspiring Their Team (and 
activating Gonks, etc).
When a Cyberpsycho refreshes after a player’s Inspire, 
they also resolve any effects that trigger when their 
team would Inspire. For instance, any Difficult actions 
become available again. 
ADDING DIFFICULTY
To add difficulty to a scenario, you can include these 
additional rules. 
CLEVER OR CRAZY?
The active player no longer decides actions between 
equal options (like focus in the same range band). Players 
randomly roll to determine these choices.
BODY HORRORS
After it activates, the Cyberpsycho will Heal 1 (always 
choosing to heal GREEN 
 GREEN tokens).
WAY OVER THE EDGE
Cyberpsychos gain additional RED 
 RED Action tokens until they 
each have four Action tokens. These tokens can't be Healed.
LETHAL WEAPONS
All the Cyberpsycho's Ranged attacks gain Deadly Crits. All 
Melee attacks targeting a Cyberpsycho gain Dangerous.
IMPLACABLE
Cyberpsychos can only be taken out by an attack that Crits. 
Non-Crit attacks can only Red-line them.
PERFECT KILLERS
Increase any keyword values (such as Rapid 2 or Stun 1) by 
+1 to actions granted by Cyberpsychosis card actions.
Solo / Co-op

## Page 201

201
In the 2070s, cyber-enhanced Edgerunners ply their trade 
in the long shadows cast by corporations like Arasaka and 
Militech. Their stories are uncountable, each unique, but 
somehow all the same. A kid with few prospects, great 
loss, and the chrome glimmer of hope. Can you lead these 
Edgerunners to fortune… or will Arasaka see them die 
legends instead?
Cyberpunk Edgerunners: Combat Zone is 100% compatible 
with Cyberpunk RED: Combat Zone.

## Page 296

Pg 5: Gang name “Combat Zoners” 
updated to “Zoners”.
Pg 5: Gang name “Neocorporate” 
updated to “Arasaka”.
Pg 6: Models now win ties against the 
Obstacle die. The text now reads:
Models are considered “the defender” 
when rolling against the Obstacle die, 
and win in the case of a tie.
Pg 7: Definitions of model’s base and 
figure added.
Models are mechanically defined in 
two parts; the figure and the base. The 
figure is the sculpture of a character 
which determines which card and 
abilities the model uses in game. The 
base is a round disk at the bottom, 
which keeps the model upright and 
is used for measuring distances and 
position mechanics. The standard [RE]
action base is 27mm in diameter, but 
some models may be issued with larger 
bases if the model requires.
Pg 10: Reflex Skill now correctly notes 
that it is used to resist both Ranged 
and Reflex actions.
Pg 16: Definition of Hacked models 
added to Basic Tech Action.
Pg 22: Vertical movement has been 
revised. Models may now jump larger 
gaps than RED 
 RED and may climb without 
starting in contact with the vertical 
barrier.
Pg 23: The following text has been 
added to the Parkour! section.
FREE MOVEMENT
When an ability causes a model to 
move, this is shorthand for granting 
the model a free basic move action. 
If the action has a color specified 
with it (such as Moves GREEN 
 GREEN 
or the “the same color”) then the 
move may be up to that range. The 
moving model must choose one of its 
action tokens (see page 19), which 
determines what die is rolled during 
parkour, but the distance moved is 
determined by the effect’s text.
Pg 23: Parkour Example with incorrect 
movement demonstration removed.
Pg 27: Shooting into a Brawl updated 
to clarify how Blast and Torrent attacks 
function when fired into a Brawl. The 
following sentence was added:
When firing a blast or torrent weapon 
into a brawl, resolve the attack against 
the target(s) as you normally would for 
those weapon types.
Pg 37: Program EB cost position 
noted.
Pg 40: Fumbling Blast Actions now 
reads: 
If you Fumble a Blast action, the 
targeted rival places the Impact token 
in base contact with the attacker 
and the Blast is resolved as a “miss” 
against the Obstacle die (instead of the 
original result). If the weapon is also 
DANGEROUS the Blast will cause an 
automatic wound to the attacker, too.
Pg 41: Rapid [X] replaces the word 
repeat with the word take: It now 
reads:
Rapid actions allow you to take the 
same action X times without using 
additional Actions.
Pg 42: Campaign Basics opening 
paragraphs edited to more generally 
explain campaigns to new players. It 
now reads:
Groups, clubs, or event organizers 
may wish to run a campaign: a series 
of games that tell a larger narrative 
about the denizens of the Combat Zone 
as you guide your team through its 
story and track its progress. Winning 
games and achieving objectives earns 
your team Street Cred. Once they’ve 
earned enough notoriety, your team 
may attempt its faction’s Prove Your 
Worth mission. It’s tough, but if you’re 
victorious, you’ve won the campaign!
Pg 43: Objective Token images added 
to sidebar.
Pg 46: Life & Death rules now include 
“Major Injury” heading with the 
following paragraph:
A model that has suffered a major 
injury might not die, but they’re not 
fighting for a while. Remove their card 
from your HQ and replace them with 
the basic version. Mercs are removed 
from your HQ, you won’t be able to use 
them until you “re-hire” them.
Pg 53: Specialist definition added, it 
reads:
Specialist — Specialists are rare 
individuals. Your team may only include 
one of this model (but may include 
other Specialist models). 
296
1ST PRINTING ERRATA
